

===== SGKT - Session Graph-Based Knowledge Tracing for Student Performance Prediction.pdf pages=12 =====

Expert Systems With Applications 206 (2022) 117681 Available online 15 June 2022 0957-4174/© 2022 Elsevier Ltd. All rights reserved. Contents lists available at ScienceDirect Expert Systems With Applications journal homepage: www.elsevier.com/locate/eswa SGKT: Session graph-based knowledge tracing for student performance prediction Zhengyang Wu a, Li Huanga, Qionghao Huangb, Changqin Huangb,∗, Yong Tangc,∗ a School of Computer Science, South China Normal University, Guangzhou, Guangdong, 510631, China b Key Laboratory of Intelligent Education Technology and Application of Zhejiang Province, Zhejiang Normal University, Jinhua, Zhejiang, 321004, China c Guangzhou Key Laboratory of Big Data and Intelligent Education, Guangzhou, Guangdong, 510631, China A R T I C L E I N F O Keywords: Knowledge tracing Graph Convolutional Network Gated Graph Neural Networks Attention Mechanism A B S T R A C T Knowledge tracing is a modeling method of students’ knowledge mastery. The deep knowledge tracing (DKT) model uses long short-term memory (LSTM) to process the sequence data of students exercises. However, the LSTM-based model pays more attention to the short-term response status of students while ignoring the long-term learning process. Moreover, existing graph-based knowledge tracing models focus on the static relationship between exercises and skills, ignoring the dynamic graphs formed by students exercises in a session. In this work, we propose a novel knowledge tracing model which is based on an exercise session graph, named session graph based knowledge tracing (SGKT). The session graph is used to model the students’ answering process. In addition, a relationship graph is used to model the relationship between exercises and skills. Then we use gated graph neural networks to obtain the students’ knowledge state from the session graph and use graph convolutional networks to obtain the embedding representations of exercises and skills in the relationship graph. Next, through the interaction mechanism, multiple interaction states composed of knowledge states and embedding representations are obtained. The attention mechanism is used to find the focus from these states and make predictions. Experiments are conducted on three publicly available datasets and the results show that our approach has advantages over some existing baseline methods. 1. Introduction Learning management systems (LMS) have been widely used in var- ious educational stages from primary to tertiary education for student administration, documentation, tracking, reporting, and the delivery of educational courses, training programs, or learning and development programs (McGill and Klobas, 2009). The development of educational data mining technology has played an important role in improving LMS (Miah et al., 2020). Monitoring students’ knowledge mastery based on their performance in completing exercises is an important task ( Toma- sevic et al., 2020) and provides a foundation for more effective learning assessment and learning recommendations (Sweeney et al., 2016). This problem, known as knowledge tracing (Liu, Shen et al. , 2021), aims to analyze the exercises that the students have worked on and infers the students’ level of knowledge mastery based on whether their answers to the exercises are correct, and then predicts the probability that students can correctly answer the exercises which will follow. Knowledge tracing was first introduced in 1994. It aims to estimate students’ learning performance in a learning guidance system ( Corbett ∗ Corresponding authors. E-mail addresses: wuzhengyang@m.scnu.edu.cn (Z. Wu), lhuang@m.scnu.edu.cn (L. Huang), qhhuang@zjnu.edu.cn (Q. Huang), cqhuang@zjnu.edu.cn (C. Huang), yongt@m.scnu.edu.cn (Y. Tang). and Anderson, 1994). After adopting the Bayesian network, it is now called Bayesian Knowledge Tracing (BKT). After that, BKT has been improved in various ways. In 2015, a recurrent neural network (RNN)- based model for student performance prediction was proposed, named Deep Knowledge Tracing (DKT) (Piech et al. , 2015). DKT adopts LSTM to perform prediction tasks since the historical records of students’ answering exercises are in the form of sequences. LSTM is a variant of RNN, which is considered a high-performance neural network for sequence processing tasks ( Graves et al. , 2013) and has been widely used in various sequence-based models, such as the methods in the field of educational data mining proposed by Xiong et al.

--- p.2 chars=8298 ---
Expert Systems With Applications 206 (2022) 117681 2 Z. Wu et al. missed in the practice of education. For example, students may give an incorrect answer to an exercise on their first attempt, however, after working on the same exercise repeatedly, they may answer it correctly, which is a sign of better knowledge mastery than if they had answered the exercise correctly the first time. Second, knowledge mastery is relatively stable over a long period, hence it is reasonable to assume that when students work on an exercise for a second time, they may remember their experience of working on it the first time. Therefore, graph-based knowledge tracing methods are proposed to solve these problems. Graph-based knowledge tracing (GKT) proposed in Nakagawa et al. (2019) uses several ways to cast the knowledge structure as a graph, where nodes correspond to skills and edges correspond to their relationships. These graphs are used as input to the model to predict the students’ responses. However, it does not analyze which kind of graph is more reasonable. In addition, some existing graph-based knowledge tracing models focus more on the relational information between exercises and skills (Yang et al., 2020). However, the graph structure information constituted by the students’ answering behaviors is ignored. In RNN-based knowledge tracing models, session sequence is the basic data for students’ knowledge state modeling. To improve the rep- resentation of session sequences, it is reasonable to structure exercise session sequences into a graph structure. This is because compared to sequences, graph structures can capture richer local dependencies through graph neural networks. Furthermore, a graph neural network is capable of providing rich local contextual information by encoding edge or node attribute features (Battaglia et al., 2018). Based on the session graph, the graph-based model can capture transitions of neighbor items and generate the latent vectors for all nodes involved in the graph. In this work, we propose a novel session graph-based knowledge tracing model named SGKT. SGKT uses gated graph neural networks (GGNN) to model the students’ knowledge state and uses graph convo- lutional network (GCN) and self-attention with a forgetting mechanism to model exercises and skills. Then, an interactive mechanism combines the student’s knowledge state with the representation of exercises and skills to obtain the final prediction result. In particular, SGKT employs a heterogeneous relation graph to represent the relationship between exercises and skills and it organizes the exercises on which each student has worked as a directed session graph. SGKT employs gated recurrent units (GRUs) to generate a hidden state of students based on a session graph because a GRU not only captures the features in the current time-step, it also integrates the features from the neighbor time-step. We conducted extensive experiments on three real datasets. The results verify the superiority of SGKT in various aspects. The contributions of this study are threefold: • This study proposes a novel graph-based knowledge tracing model that uses a session graph to model students’ knowledge states. • To better simulate a scenario in which students answer exercises and for model knowledge tracing, this study applies heteroge- neous relation graphs and forgetting mechanisms and develops a more powerful graph-based solution. • This study verifies the robustness of the knowledge tracing solu- tion based on the session graph structure model compared with the current mainstream solutions through various experiments. The rest of the paper is organized as follows. We review the related work in Section 2. In Section 3, we introduce some important nota- tions and preliminaries. In Section 4, we describe the framework and implementation of SGKT. Extensive experimental studies are detailed in Section 5. Finally, we present the conclusion in Section 6. 2. Related work In this section, we first review the related work on knowledge tracing. Then, the related research on graph-based knowledge tracing is summarized. 2.1. Knowledge tracing The term knowledge tracing was first proposed by Corbett and An- derson in 1995. A knowledge tracing model comprises three parts: (1) student knowledge state modeling; (2) exercises and skills (or knowl- edge concepts) modeling; (3) prediction module. In the earliest knowl- edge tracing model, the probability estimation of students’ learni

--- p.3 chars=6778 ---
Expert Systems With Applications 206 (2022) 117681 3 Z. Wu et al. ℎ𝑣 ∈ 𝑅𝑠 which contains the information of the neighborhood for each node. The state embeddingℎ𝑣 is a𝑠-dimension vector of node𝑣 and can produce an output 𝑜𝑣 such as the node label. In application, the GNN retains a state that can represent information from its neighborhood with arbitrary depth. A typical application of GNN is node classifica- tion. Essentially, every node in the graph is associated with a label, and we want to predict the nodes’ labels without ground truths. In the node classification task setup, each node 𝑣 is characterized by its feature 𝑥𝑣 and associated with a ground-truth label 𝑡𝑣. Given a partially labeled graph𝐺, the goal is to leverage these labeled nodes to predict the labels of the unlabeled. It learns to represent each node with a 𝑑-dimensional vector (hidden state) ℎ𝑣, containing its neighborhood information. GCN (Kipf and Welling , 2017) updates node representations based on itself and its neighbors and is often proposed for semi-supervised graph classification. Using multiple graph convolutional layers, GCN can ensure the updated nodes represent the attributes of neighbor nodes and the information of higher-order neighbors. To reduce the limitations in the GNN model and improve the long- term propagation of information across the graph structure, GGNN ( Li et al., 2016) uses a gate mechanism in the propagation step and unrolls the recurrence for a fixed number of steps𝑇 and uses back-propagation through time to compute the gradient. 2.3. Graph-based knowledge tracing Recently, research on knowledge tracing has focused on exploring the use of graph structures to capture more information. The model proposed by Chanaa and Faddouli (2020) first constructs a dynamic graph that changes over time, where each node represents a student, the nodes are fixed, but the edges are dynamic. Both nodes and graph topology are transforming over time, matching the knowl- edge tracing of students. The model learns feature representation by aggregating each student (i.e., node) and its neighbors, then extracting the network topology information at each different time step. Finally, the representation of each node is sent to the classifier to determine the student’s knowledge state. Nakagawa et al. ( 2019) proposed GKT. This model sets the course exercises as a graph structure 𝐺 = (𝑉,𝐸 ). The 𝑛 skills are the nodes 𝑉 = { 𝑣1, …,𝑣𝑛} of the graph, and the shared dependency of these skills is the edge 𝐸 ⊆ 𝑉 × 𝑉 of the graph. Assume that the student independently has the knowledge state ℎ𝑡 = { ℎ𝑡 𝑣𝑖 } at that time for each skill at time 𝑡, and the knowledge state is updated with time as follows: when answering the exercises related to the skill𝑣𝑖, the student’s knowledge state is not only the skill of the answer itself, it also contains information on its neighbor skills; then, the updated knowledge state is used to predict the probability of the student correctly answering each skill at 𝑡. GIKT proposed by Yang et al. (2020) considers the exercise–skill relationship graph a bipartite graph and then uses the GCN to fully integrate the exercise–skill corre- lation through embedding propagation. PEBG-KT proposed by Liu et al. (2020) employs a pre-training approach to learn a low-dimensional embedding via the exercise–skill Bipartite Graph for each exercise with useful side information. However, the model proposed by Chanaa and Faddouli ( 2020) and GKT ignore the relationship between exercises and skills. GIKT and PEBG-KT use bipartite graphs which focus on the relationship between exercises and skills but ignore the relationship between exercises. Furthermore, they all ignore the graph structure formed during the students’ exercise answering sessions. 3. Preliminaries In this section, we first formally define the task of student perfor- mance prediction in an intelligent learning system. Then, we describe how to represent a heterogeneous relation graph about the students, exercises, and skills. Next, we explain the construction of the session graph. Expert Systems With Applications 206 (2022) 117681 4 Z. Wu et al. Fig. 2. A session-graph example. Definition 4 (Forgetting Mechanism ). In the process of learning, for- getting is inevitable. Due to forgetting, the exercises that students have answered correctly may be answered incorrectly after a period. Furthermore, the forgetting rate is positively correlated with the length of time. We formalize the forgetting mechanism (Chen et al., 2021) as: 𝑚(𝛥𝜏) = 𝜃1 ×𝑒𝑥𝑝−𝜃2𝛥𝜏 +𝜃3, (1) where𝜃1,𝜃2, and𝜃3 are the fitting parameters and𝑚(𝛥𝜏) represents the residual memory ratio. A higher𝑚(𝛥𝜏) means that the student has more memory about the corresponding content. 𝛥𝜏 is the time step between the current moment and the initial moment. 4. Session graph-based knowledge tracing model To address the problem defined in Section 3 , we propose SGKT, a session graph-based knowledge tracing framework. As shown in Fig. 3, SGKT comprises three parts: the HRG embedding module (for exercises and skills modeling), the SG embedding module (for student knowl- edge state modeling), and the prediction module. The HRG embedding module is used to learn the embedding of exercise-to-exercise and skill-to-skill relational information from HRG. It generates the represen- tation vector, and then employs self-attention mechanisms with FM to add dynamic semantics to them. The SG embedding module is used to generate the students’ knowledge state. The prediction module predicts the probability of students answering the next exercise correctly. The SG embedding and prediction modules are jointly optimized during training, where the exercises and skills embeddings are dynamically obtained from the HRG embedding module. 4.1. HRG embedding module This module uses the GCN model to generate the embedding repre- sentation of the nodes in the HRG composed of students, exercises, and skills. Before embedding HRG, we first define neighbors according to the meta relationship path in Definition 2, where three meta-relational paths are defined, namely eSe, eKe, and kEk. Specifically, we process the dataset according to the following rules: the neighbors of an ex- ercise are the exercises answered by the same student, i.e., eSe, or the exercises that require the same skill, i.e., eKe. A skill’s neighbor is that skill covered by the same exercise, i.e., kEk. Therefore, during the propagation of the GCN, we use two matrices, one is the exercise– exercise relation matrix, and the other is the exercise–skill relation matrix. As shown in the upper part of Fig. 3 , although HRG is a tripartite graph, the embedding layer of this module only focuses on the state of exercises and skills, and students are regarded as a connection between exercises. In the matrices of exercises and skills propagated in GCN, the neighbors of an exercise are the other exercises answered by the same student and related skills. The neighbors of skills are all the exercises related to it. To extract the high-order information, we use GCN to generate the embedding of exercises and skills i.e., ∼ 𝑒 and ∼ 𝑘. GCN has a lot of graph convolution layers to encode high-order neighbor information, and each layers’ node can be updated by states of itself and neighbor nodes. We set the 𝑖th node in the graph as 𝛽𝑖, indicating skill state 𝑘𝑖 or exercise state 𝑒𝑖. We also set 𝛽𝑖’s neighbor nodes as 𝐍𝑖. So the exercise of an𝑙th layer of GCN can be expressed as: 𝛽𝑙 𝑖 =𝑅𝑒𝐿𝑈 ( 1 𝐍𝑖 ∑ 𝑗∈{𝑖}∪𝐍𝑖 𝐲𝑙𝛽𝑙−1 𝑗 + 𝐳𝑙 ) , (2) where 𝐲𝑙 and 𝐳𝑙 are the aggregate weight and bias, which can be learned in the 𝑙th GCN layer, and the activation function ReLU is used in it. After embedding the propagation by GCN, we can obtain the ∼ 𝑒 and ∼ 𝑘. 4.2. SG embedding module This embedding module uses the GGNN model to generate students’ knowledge hidden states based on the session graph. 4.2.1. Knowledge hidden state During the exercises and corresponding answers propagated in GGNN, they are combined in the following way: 𝑐𝑖 =𝑒𝑖⊕𝑎𝑖, (3) where 𝑒𝑖 denotes the 𝑖th exercise in the session and ⊕ indicates an element-wise addition. The process of information propagation in GGNN is shown in Fig. 4.𝛼𝑖 is a new state, being the input of the GRU which contains information about the neighbor nodes of the current node𝑐𝑖, which indicates the effect of the previous exercise on the next exercise in the exercise session. 𝛼𝑖 can be formalized as: 𝛼𝑖 = 𝐂𝐨𝐧𝐜𝐚𝐭(𝐌𝐈 𝐢 ([𝑐1,𝑐 2, …,𝑐𝑛 ] 𝐖𝐈 + 𝐛𝐈), 𝐌𝐨 𝐢 ([𝑐1,𝑐 2, …,𝑐𝑛 ] 𝐖𝐨 + 𝐛𝐨)), (4) where 𝐖𝐼, 𝐖𝑂 ∈ 𝑅𝑑×𝑑 are the parameter m

An example of information propagation between layer 𝑡 − 1 and layer 𝑡 of GGNN where the solid line represents the post-order relationship and the dashed line represents the pre-order relationship. 4.3. Self-attention with FM module Self-attention with a forgetting mechanism is used to model the exercises and skills. Based on the HRG, we believe that GCN can gen- erate the embedding representation of the static relationship between exercises and skills. However, this embedding representation does not contain the semantics information of students’ exercise behavior. There- fore, in this module, a self-attention mechanism is used to learn the semantics of students’ exercises and answering behaviors to enhance the modeling of exercises and skills. We denote the globe exercises answering states by 𝐸 ={ 𝑒𝑖 |||𝑖 ∈ [1, 2, …,𝑡 − 1] } . Then, the self-attention mechanism is used to obtain the student’s exercise answering state 𝐹. This process is formalized as: 𝐹 =𝑆𝑜𝑓𝑡𝑚𝑎𝑥 ( (𝐸𝑊 𝑄)(𝐸𝑊 𝐾 )𝑇 √ 𝑑𝑘 )(𝐸𝑊 𝑉 ), (10) where 𝑊𝑄, 𝑊𝐾, 𝑊𝑉 ∈ 𝑅𝑑×𝑑 are the projection matrices, 𝐸 ∈ 𝑅2𝑑×𝑑, and √ 𝑑𝑘 is the scaling factor, which reduces the influence of a signifi- cant increase in the magnitude of the dot product.

To enhance the personalization of answering behavior informa- tion to students’ answering state, we add a layer of the forgetting mechanism. This process is formalized as: ̂𝐸 = {𝑓𝑖(𝜃1 ×𝑒𝑥𝑝−𝜃2𝛥𝜏𝑖 +𝜃3)|𝑖 ∈ {1, 2, …,𝑡 − 1}}, (11) where 𝑓𝑖 ∈ 𝐹, 𝛥𝜏𝑖 indicates the time-step interval between the 𝑖th moment and the initial moment. 𝑓𝑖 is the vector generated by Eq. (10) based on the elements in𝐸 at each time step. At the𝑖th time step, after adjusting the forgetting weight of 𝑓𝑖 through Eq. (11), input Eq. (10) again to get 𝑓𝑖+1. Similarly, after adjusting the forgetting weight using Eq. (11), 𝑓𝑖+1 is also input again into Eq. (10) to get 𝑓𝑖+2. Iterate 𝑡 − 1 times according to this, until all𝑓𝑖 has completed the adjustment of the forgetting weight and they constitute ̂𝐸. 4.4. Prediction module This module predicts the probability of answering the next exercise correctly. In this module, the skills embedding ∼ 𝑘𝑗 ∈ 𝐾𝑡, the answered exercises embedding ∼ 𝑒𝑖 ∈ ̂𝐸𝑡, and the embedding of target exercise ∼ 𝑒𝑡 are combined as the exercise knowledge state . The ℎ𝑡 of the GGNN generated is the student knowledge state . These states are interacted by element-wise addition. The details are as follows: Tuple ⟨ ∼ 𝑒𝑡,ℎ𝑡⟩ indicates the mastery degree of the target exercise. ⟨ ∼ 𝑘𝑗,ℎ𝑡⟩ indicates the mastery degree of the student on the 𝑗th skill, which is related to the target exercise.⟨ ∼ 𝑒𝑡, ̂𝐸𝑡⟩ and ⟨ ∼ 𝑘𝑗, ̂𝐸𝑡⟩ represent the influence of the student’s exercise answering experience on the target exercise and the associated skills in the target exercise. Then, we use an attention mechanism to obtain the weights of all interaction terms and compute the weighted sum to predict: 𝛾𝑖,𝑗 =𝑆𝑜𝑓𝑡𝑚𝑎𝑥𝑖,𝑗 (𝐖𝑇 [𝐟𝑖, 𝐟𝑗 ] +𝑏), (12) 𝑝𝑡 = ∑ 𝐟𝑖∈ ̂𝐸∪{ℎ𝑡} ∑ 𝐟𝑗 ∈ ∼ 𝐾𝑒𝑡 ∪{ ∼ 𝑒𝑡} 𝛾𝑖,𝑗 𝐠(𝐟𝑖, 𝐟𝑗 ), (13) where 𝐖 ∈ 𝑅𝑑×𝑑 is the parameter matrix, and 𝑏 ∈ 𝑅𝑑 is the bias vector.𝑝𝑡 represents the predicted probability of students answering the exercise𝑒𝑡 correctly. In the Eq. (13), ∼ 𝐾𝑒𝑡 is the aggregated neighbor skill embedding of the exercise𝑒𝑡, and function 𝐠 represents the dot product. Finally, Gradient Descent is used to minimize the cross-entropy loss between the predicted probability of answering correctly, which can be formalized as: 𝐿 = − ∑ 𝑡 (𝛾𝑡𝑙𝑜𝑔𝑝𝑡 +(1 −𝛾𝑡 )𝑙𝑜𝑔(1 −𝑝𝑡 )), (14) where 𝛾𝑡 represents the truth label. 5. Experiments In this section, we describe the extensive experiments that were conducted to verify the effectiveness and advantage of our approach. We compare the performance of SGKT 1 with several baselines on three real datasets. To compare the performance between the sequence-based and graph-based KT model, we conduct the knowledge state evolution experiment and the cold start experiment using our approaches, DKT and DKT+. Finally, we conducted two ablation studies to observe the core module and the key super-parameter of our approach. 1 https://github.com/lhuang65/SGKT Table 2 Datasets statistics. Items ASSIST09 ASSIST12 EdNet # students 3852 27,485 5000 # exercises 17,737 53,065 12,161 # skills 123 265 189 # exercises per skill 173 200 147 # skills per exercise 1.197 1.000 2280 # attempts per exercise 16 51 56 # attempts per skill 2743 10,224 8420 5.1. Overview of datasets Our experiments are performed on three classic knowledge tracing application datasets. The detailed statistics of these datasets are shown in Table 2. ASSIST09 was collected during the school year 2009–2010 from AS- SISTments online education platform. We conducted our experiments on the skill builder dataset. Similar to Xiong et al. (2016), we removed the redundant data from the original dataset. As a result, the dataset has 3852 students, 123 skills, and 17,737 exercises. ASSIST12 was collected from the same platform as ASSIST09 during the school year 2012–2013. Each exercise is related to one skill in this dataset, but one skill is still related to more than one exercise. We undertake the same data processing as for ATTIST09. The dataset has 27,485 students, 2,709,436 exercises, and 265 skills. EdNet was collected by Choi et al. (2020) from which we randomly chose 5000 students, 189 skills, and 12,161 exercises. We used 80 percent of the data as training sets and 20 percent as test sets for each dataset. We used AUC as the evaluation metric to evaluate the performance of these datasets. 5.2. Performance In SGKT, we set the embedding size of exercise and skill to 100. All parameter matrices are randomly initialized and they can be updated during the training process. In the whole process, we set the hidden size of the student’s knowledge state to 100. In the embedding propagation module, we set the aggregated layers to 3. The learning and dropout rates are set to 0.00025 and 0.8, respectively, to prevent overfitting. The values of three parameters in the forgetting mechanism (i.e., 𝜃1, 𝜃2, and 𝜃3) are randomly selected within the interval ( −0.1, 0.1). After extensive experiments, we found it most suitable to set the batch size to 6. In the process of the attention network, it is experimentally shown that considering exercise similarity is more effective than considering skill similarity. We also set the attribute bound to 0.7 to filter irrelevant embeddings in the attention network. Table 3 and Fig. 5 show that SGKT offers better AUC performance than the baseline methods. 5.4. Knowledge state evolution description Knowledge state evolution description, that is, tracing the level of students’ skills, is an important application of knowledge tracing models (Piech et al. , 2015). The description of knowledge states can visually show the strength and weakness of each student’s mastery level of each skill. Students can quickly find the shortcomings in their knowledge and be more motivated to actively address this using the knowledge state evolution depiction. Figs. 6–8 show the heat-map of a student in the dataset ASSIST09 whose knowledge state is constantly changing on certain skills. Figs. 6– 8 illustrate the difference in the knowledge state evolution using SGKT, DKT, and DKT+ in predicting a student’s mastery level on four skills. In these three figures, the label in the vertical dimension corre- sponds to the skills. To show the results clearly, we randomly chose four skills (ID: 14, 64, 80, and 114) in those answered by the student. The horizontal dimension shows a sequence of knowledge concepts and answers (0 indicates incorrectly answered and 1 indicates correctly answered) from the test set, where the labels refer to the knowledge concepts input into the model at each time step. The heat-map color indicates the predicted probability that the student will demonstrate the corresponding skill correctly in the next step. The darker the color, the higher the probability. As Figs. 6 and 8 show, after answering the 26 exercises, the student almost masters these four skills, but the student’s mastery level fluctuates during the learning process shown in Fig. 8 . However, as Fig. 7 shows, the student masters skills 64 and 114 but fails to master skills 14 and 80. We can also observe from Figs. 6 –8 that SGKT has a better knowl- edge state performance than DKT and DKT +. The knowledge state prediction using SGKT is more stable than DKT and DKT +. Second, since the HRG Embedding Module enhances the association between exercises, between skills, and between exercises and skills, an exercise which requires only one skill can affect the student’s mastery level of other skills. For example, when the student repeatedly fails to demonstrate skill 80, the prediction accuracy for all skills decreases. But after the student correctly demonstrates skill 80 four consecutive times, the prediction accuracy for all skills gradually increases. On the contrary, DKT and DKT + do not exhibit this phenomenon. In real life, such as unit exams, although different exercises may require different skills, there are potential connections among skills. Therefore, SGKT is better able to simulate reality. Figs. 6–8 only describe the difference in knowledge state prediction stability on SGKT, DKT, and DKT+. To give a better illustration, we fur- ther compare the overall waviness of the knowledge state prediction on SGKT, DKT, and DKT+ at each time step in an exercise session. We plot the change in the knowledge state prediction of these four skills (ID: 14, 64, 80, and 114) using SGKT, DKT, and DKT + in Fig. 9 to display the prediction waviness distribution at ea

Comparison of the waviness of the knowledge state prediction using DKT, SGKT, and DKT + in the test set. that the knowledge state prediction curve of SGKT is smoother than that of DKT and DKT +. Prediction waviness is defined as follows: for an individual student, prediction waviness can be obtained according to the observed input and the change in the corresponding prediction at two different time steps. We denote prediction waviness by𝛥𝑖 𝑡, which is obtained from Eq. (15) : 𝛥𝑖 𝑡 = |𝑦𝑖 𝑡 −𝑦𝑖 𝑡−1|, (15) where 𝑦𝑖 𝑡−1 and 𝑦𝑖 𝑡 represent the knowledge state prediction of 𝑖th skill at time-step 𝑡 and 𝑡 − 1. Fig. 10 describes the knowledge state prediction waviness distri- bution of all students in the test set on DKT, SGKT, and DKT + using boxplots. A boxplot is a graph with a good indication of how the data are spread. It is a standardized way of displaying a dataset based on a five-number summary: the minimum, the maximum, median, and the first and third quartiles. A boxplot can visually show the distribution of numerical data by displaying the data quartiles and averages. From Fig. 10, we can observe that the prediction waviness distributions of DKT and DKT + are more comprehensive than that of SGKT, and their medians are also higher than our proposed approach, indicating that the prediction ability of our approach is more stable than DKT and DKT+. 5.5. Cold start Knowledge tracing also faces the cold start issue. Referring to the literature (Zhao et al. , 2020), we study the performance in two cold start scenarios, i.e., training with data of a few students and new students with short exercise sequences . • scenario 1: Training with data of a few students is to train the knowledge tracing model with as few students’ interactions as possible and applies it to new unseen samples. • scenario 2: New students with short exercise sequences occurs when new students enter the online learning system and the lack of exercise records result in insufficient characteristics to input into the knowledge tracing model. In scenario 1 Training with data of few students , we conducted exper- iments with different student numbers ranging from 0.1 to 15 percent of the training dataset to compare the prediction performance of SGKT, DKT, and DKT +. As shown in Fig. 11 , SGKT achieves better perfor- mance than DKT on the ASSIST12 and Ednet datasets and performs better than DKT+ on the Ednet dataset. On ASSIST09, the performance of SGKT is lower than DKT before the students’ data reaches 7% and is lower than DKT+ before the students’ data reaches 10%. On ASSIST12, the performance of SGKT is lower than DKT+ before the students’ data reaches 2%. The reason for this is that the number of repeated exercises in the exercise session is small in this dataset. In scenario 2 New students with short exercise sequences , we first cat- egorize the training data into eight groups, each with a different range of exercise sequence lengths, i.e., (0, 5], (0, 10], (0, 15], (0, 20], (0, 25], (0, 50], (0, 75], and (0, 100]. Each exercise sequence in the training data is constructed by intercepting the corresponding lengths of exercises from the original exercise sequence. It is easy to see that the case for the first group is the most difficult, since students from this group have the fewest exercise answering records. We compare the performance of the different methods in Fig. 12. In this scenario, SGKT achieves better performance than DKT and DKT+ on the ASSIST09 and Ednet datasets. When the length of the sequence of the exercises is less than (0, 50], the performance of SGKT on the ASSIST12 dataset is lower than that of DKT and DKT+. The reason for this is that the continuous exercise sequence of students in the testing data is usually very long (the average length is 99.1), which leads to insufficient construction of the session graph due to the short exercise sequence data and during the training of SGKT. 5.6. Conclusion and future work In this work, we propose a session graph-based knowledge tracing model. Our approach builds a directed graph using students’ exercise- answer records and builds a heterogeneous relation graph of exercises and skills. Then, we apply GGNN and GCN to generate the student’s knowledge state, which contains the local context information of the

--- p.11 chars=5691 ---
Expert Systems With Applications 206 (2022) 117681 11 Z. Wu et al. Fig. 13. Comparison of the impact of knowledge states of differing hidden sizes. exercise-answer record and the embedding representation of all exer- cises and skills. Next, we use the self-attention network with a forget- ting mechanism to capture the global dependencies between distant locations. Finally, we combine short-term dynamics (the most recently answered exercise) and the dependence of global self-attention to ex- press the exercise answer sequence in a linear mechanism. We validate the advantages of SGKT on several real-world datasets used in knowl- edge tracing, compared to some existing methods in terms of AUC, knowledge state evolution description and cold start. Furthermore, we conduct an ablation study. Knowledge tracing is a research hotspot in the field of educational data mining. Traditional knowledge tracing methods take exercise an- swer history as a sequence, ignoring the relationships between the exercises formed during the answering process. In this work, we im- proved the knowledge tracing model to understand the knowledge state from the exercise session graph node information. Cold start is a new problem in current knowledge tracing research. Although this work considers the cold start issue in the experiments, our model did not perform any optimization and more analysis is needed. We will continue to work on this problem in the future. ===== HHSKT - A Learner-Question Interactions Based Heterogeneous Graph Neural Network Model for Knowledge Tracing.pdf pages=13 =====

Expert Systems With Applications 215 (2023) 119334 Available online 23 November 2022 0957-4174/© 2022 Elsevier Ltd. All rights reserved. Contents lists available at ScienceDirect Expert Systems With Applications journal homepage: www.elsevier.com/locate/eswa HHSKT: A learner–question interactions based heterogeneous graph neural network model for knowledge tracing✩ Qin Ni a, Tingjiang Wei a,∗, Jiabao Zhao b, Liang He b, Chanjin Zheng c,∗ a Institute of Artificial Intelligence on Education, Shanghai Normal University, Shanghai, 201418, China b National Intelligent Society Governance Experimental Base (Education), East China Normal University, Shanghai, 200062, China c Department of Educational Psychology and Shanghai Institute of AI in Education, East China Normal University, Shanghai, 200062, China A R T I C L E I N F O Keywords: Intelligent education Knowledge tracing Graph neural network Educational data mining A B S T R A C T Knowledge tracing (KT) has evolved into a crucial component of the online education system with the rapid development of online adaptive learning. A key component of the online education system, knowledge tracing (KT) assesses the state of knowledge by tracing each learner’s learning activities. The deep KT model, however, is unable to completely extract the features of the questions and skills due to the heterogeneity of the knowledge structure and the sparsity of the interaction records. The model’s capacity to handle diverse data is also restricted by over parameterization. Additionally, rather than focusing solely on a precise fit, Intelligent Tutoring System (ITS) should stress interpretable feedback to the learner. The deep KT approach’s item parameters are still unable to give students useful feedback. This paper proposes to trace learner’s short-term attentional knowledge based on heterogeneous hierarchical differentiation, named HHSKT. Hierarchical heterogeneous knowledge structures and short-term memory enhancement will be used to model the effects of different interaction sequences on learners. Specifically, knowledge structure features are extracted by constructing a heterogeneous graph-based graph information augmentation component. Question differentiation parameters are derived by transforming the TrueSkill system. Besides, learners’ history-related practices are emphasized by windowing attention. Comparing regression-based and deep-based knowledge tracing experiments shows that HHSKT significantly outperforms the state-of-the-art approach on three real- world benchmark datasets (with an average AUC improvement of up to 3%), demonstrating the superiority of the proposed model. 1. Introduction In recent years, online schooling on a massive scale has exploded, especially following the pandemic. Simultaneously, homogeneous on- line learning content cannot satisfy the individualized learning needs of a growing number of users ( Injadat, Moubayed, Nassif, & Shami , 2020; Lan & Baraniuk , 2016). Current online learning platforms lack the adaptability to address these difficulties and provide learners with more specific, individualized feedback and help. The COVID-19-related school closures pushed schools to move instruction online and pro- vide remote support for students ( Boltz, Yadav, Dillman, & Robertson , 2021). Therefore, the traditional method of measuring the knowledge of learners’ based on test scores and written responses lacks the nec- essary tools and environments to promote high-quality and thoughtful remote learning. ✩ This work was sponsored by the National Natural Science Foundation of China under Grant 6210020445. Shanghai Municipal Natural Science Foundation, China under Grant 21ZR1446900, 21511100102. ∗ Corresponding authors. E-mail addresses: niqin@shnu.edu.cn (Q. Ni), mxdlzg@163.com (T. Wei), jiabao.zhao@ica.stc.sh.cn (J. Zhao), lhe@cs.ecnu.edu.cn (L. He), chjzheng@dep.ecnu.edu.cn (C. Zheng). Data analysis and intelligent tutoring systems rely heavily on edu- cational data mining (de Andrade, Rigo, & Barbosa, 2021). Knowledge Tracing (KT) is a learning situation analysis task based on computer- assisted large-scale data processing capabilities to trace learning activ- ities, which can deliver diagnostic results without sophisticated human engagement for learners and teachers (Minn, Vie, Takeuchi, Kashima, & Zhu, 2021). Knowledge components (KCs) constitute learning content, and a learner’s knowledge states (KS) are a collection of KCs. As demonstrated in Fig. 1, 

--- p.2 chars=7131 ---
Expert Systems With Applications 215 (2023) 119334 2 Q. Ni et al. Fig. 1. An illustration of exercising process. The exercising process of a learner and the corresponding KS of each exercise. Bhatt, Thille, Zimmaro and Gattani , 2020 ), logistic regression meth- ods (Vie & Kashima , 2019), and deep learning methods ( Piech et al. , 2015). Psychometric approaches based on psychology (based on statis- tical regression, etc.) and Bayesian-based methods are typical examples of traditional methods ( Ha, Hong, Hwang, & Yoon , 2018 ). Item re- sponse theory (IRT) is commonly employed in the KT framework ( Su et al. , 2021 ), where Rasch (1PL-IRT) is also employed to estimate item difficulty (Ghosh, Heffernan, & Lan, 2020). Some recurrent neural network (RNN) based methods have been proposed to model long dependencies between interactions ( Ding & Larson , 2020; Lin & Chi , 2017; Piech et al. , 2015), and transformer-based methods have been proposed to emphasize the weights of different KCs (Oya & Morishima, 2021; Pandey & Karypis , 2019 ; Pu, Yudelson, Ou, & Huang , 2020 ). These deep models have demonstrated impressive performance thanks to their robust fitting capabilities. Deep knowledge tracing (DKT) is an early RNN-based deep KT model ( Piech et al. , 2015 ), while self-attentive knowledge tracing (SAKT) is the first model to integrate the transformer structure into KT ( Pandey & Karypis , 2019 ). The RNN structure may step-by-step process sequential input from left to right and from bottom to top, retaining specific information of memory units depending on gating information and retaining the information recorded during steps 𝑡0 to 𝑡𝑛−1 to forecast 𝑡𝑛 (Graves, rahman Mohamed, & Hinton, 2013). Online learning data consist mostly of sequential information, which is ideally suited for processing by RNN architectures. SAKT proposes an attention mechanism based on the sequential task in order to highlight the con- tribution of various KCs to the prediction of KC mastery at the present time. In addition to aforementioned models, graph neural networks (GNNs) have garnered significant attention ( Veliković et al. , 2017 ), and many graph construction methods have been proposed for models such as graph-based interaction knowledge tracing (GIKT) (Yang et al., 2021). Furthermore, the aggregation and representation capabilities of graph networks have been applied to significantly improve the prediction capability and interpretability of KT models substantially. Table 1 outlines the primary work and limits of the most prevalent KT techniques now in use, including some cutting-edge technologies. Despite the fact that, current deep KT models are superior to tradi- tional models for predicting learners’ mastery of KC, the existing models have three major flaws. First, the relationships between items, skills, and learners in knowledge tracing datasets are often quite complex. In- sufficient research exists on combining diverse features and large-scale learner interaction network architectures. Secondly, interpretable item parameters are frequently absent from deep model. It is more important for ITS to provide learners with practical and solvable feedback than model fitting capabilities. Lastly, DKT’s modeling of learners’ memory skills relies solely on gated information, which is insufficiently inter- pretable. The transformer-based approach can assign various weights to sequential characteristics. Attention has visual explanatory advantages, but nevertheless resists the memory process of the learner. This work proposes HHSKT, a heterogeneous GNN-based model for multi-level summarization of knowledge structures and learner atten- tion reinforcement. Higher-order relationships between items and skills are extracted by heterogeneous GNN, a rich representation of skill-level information is obtained by heterogeneous graph coarsening, and the final prediction is completed by item differentiation in conjunction with the attention mechanism of window sequence memory. The following is a summary of our key contributions in this pa- per: (1) The model implements a higher-order item skill embedding representation of heterogeneous learner interaction graphs based on massive amounts of data. (2) The model transforms the TrueSkill system to obtain interpretable parameters. Explainable item differences and item difficulty are generated by generating learners with explain- able feedback. (3) The model’s windowed attention is superior to the mechanis

The feature dimension is small and sensitive to the time factor. AFM (Cen, Koedinger, & Junker, 2006) Self-Explaining Factor analysis methods were further developed to interpret the model predictions regarding statistical characteristics. Fewer characteristic factors were used. PFA (Pavlik, Cen, & Koedinger, 2009) Self-Explaining Fewer characteristic factors were used. KTM (Vie & Kashima, 2019) Self-Explaining KTMs use a sparse set of weights for all features to model the learners’ answer outcome. The feature calculation is complex and has a cold start problem. DASH (Lindsey, Shroyer, Pashler, & Mozer, 2014) Self-Explaining The DASH model is involved in memory forgetting modeling and factor analysis, where DASH stands for difficulty, ability, and student interaction history, respectively. Inadequate response to multi-skilled problems. DAS3H (Choffin, Popineau, Bourda, & Vie, 2019) Self-Explaining It combines aspects of IRT and PFA and extends the DASH model to calculate feature factors using a time-window-based counting function. The window calculation is complex and has limited performance improvement. Deep-IRT (Yeung, 2019) Partially interpretable. For the first time, item response theory (IRT) is combined with a knowledge tracing model based on a deep neural network structure of dynamic key–value memory network (DKVMN). Due to the fixed binary Q-matrix designed by the expert, it lacks a personalized description of the student at the input level. GKT (Nakagawa, Iwasawa, & Matsuo, 2019) Black box model, graph structure can be interpreted based on local features. First use of graph neural network methods in the KT domain. Lack of ability to deal with multiple concepts and complex relationships between conceptual problems. GIKT (Yang et al., 2020) The problem of data sparsity is solved by using GCN to aggregate embedding representations under concept and exercise relations. The model is complex, the training scale is large, and it is not pedagogically interpretable. PEBG (Liu et al., 2020) Graph-based pre-trained skill features are used to enrich the feature information used by the model. The features of the graph structure are not well taken into account. HGKT (Tong, Wang, Liu, Zhou and Han, 2020) Black box models, interpreting models by interpreting structured features. Hierarchical exercise graphs are constructed based on hierarchical relationships between exercises (direct and indirect relationships). Knowledge concepts are divided into different granularities and are not sufficiently hierarchical. JKT (Song et al., 2021) The connection of exercises across concepts is established to capture high-level semantic information while increasing the interpretability of the model. Insufficient consideration of the directionality of prerequisites between knowledge and the correlation information between nodes. SKT (Tong, Liu et al., 2020) A propagation model is introduced to model the propagation of different knowledge point cascades of influence. SAKT (Pandey & Karypis, 2019) Black box model with local interpretation based on attention. For the first time in the KT domain, the transformer model is used to model student interaction history based on a self-attentive mechanism. Other features around the attention of the nodes are suppressed and the order between exercises is not taken into account. Expert Systems With Applications 215 (2023) 119334 4 Q. Ni et al. based on ensemble learning is another essential KT subdiscipline (Zia et al., 2021). However, ensemble learning is a stable learning tech- nique and the variety of individual students must be increased (Jiang et al., 2021; Jiang, Yu, Gong, & Du, 2022). In addition, deep models provide excellent interpretability when integrated with factor analysis models, such as, Deep-IRT and NeuralCD, which combine IRT with deep approaches (Wang et al., 2020; Yeung, 2019). The majority of data in the area of education are non-Euclidean structured graph data, and numerous approaches have been developed to handle graph-structured data (Veliković et al., 2017; Zhao, Song et al., 2020). Graph convolutional networks (GCN) are inspired by CNN ideas to update their own representation based on neighboring nodes on graph-structured input, which may be aggregated to higher- order neighbor information via multi-layer convolution (Schlichtkrull et al., 2018). GNN has been implemented in several disciplines, in- cluding natural language processing (NLP), recommendation systems, and others. Ahale, Pattanshetti, and Nayak (2021) and Schlichtkrull, Cao, and Titov (2021). There are GNN applications in the KT domain, such as graph knowledge tracing (GKT) (Nakagawa et al., 2019), but they do not take learner characteristics into account. A heterogeneous graph neural network is used in HHSKT to extract higher-order repre- sentations of model inputs and to ensure the performance of the model on large-scale data through subgraph sampling. Hierarchical features are the information underlying the learner’s educational process, and hierarchical information is crucial to the educational process (Shuai, Li, Liu, & Shen, 2022). Clustering in hierarchical graph KT (HGKT) (Tong, Wang et al., 2020) introduces hierarchical aspects of items, although hierarchical features produced by threshold clustering do not ade- quately capture hierarchical features of items. One technique utilizes undulating edge matching heuristic, while the other coarsening method picks a subset of nodes from the original graph to create a coarse graph node set (Ma & Chen, 2021). A heterogeneous graph neural network is used in HHSKT to extract higher-order representations of model inputs and to ensure the performance of the model on large-scale data through subgraph sampling. 2.2. TrueSkill system The TrueSkill rating system is primarily designed to evaluate gamers as a derivation of the Glicko scoring system (Herbrich, Minka, & Graepel, 2007; Minka, Cleven, & Zaykov, 2018; Quispe & Luna, 2015), but its excellent rating capabilities are equally applicable to mea- suring learning capacity in non-gaming situations (Lee, 2019). The TrueSkill methodology is superior to the PageRank method for pre- dicting question difficulty in community Q&A (Wang, Liu, Wang, & Guo, 2014), such as stackoverflow’s question difficulty classification. In a large-scale speaker-likability rating test, Baumann showed the re- liability of TrueSkill method utilizing score rankings (Baumann, 2017). In social scenarios, students interacted with graph-based scenarios to develop practical interpersonal skills in unexpected contexts. TrueSkill can reliably predict the decisions made by students in simulated social situations (Kawatsu, Hubal, & Marinier, 2018). The preceding study illustrates the consistency and precision of the TrueSkill method. The origins of item response theory lie in the field of psychometrics. It posits that there is an underlying trait that reflects each learner’s ability and that this attribute may be detected through the learner’s item responses. Item response theory (IRT) consists of two factors, ability 𝜃 and difficulty 𝑏𝑖, with the higher item’s difficulty matching to the higher the learner’s necessary ability (Harvey & Hammer, 1999). By clearly defining factors such as item difficulty and student competence, the model’s computational process is visible and easily interpretable. However, IRT assumes that students’ skills remain constant over time, which is inappropriate for long-term knowledge tracing. Rasch model may be considered as a 1PL-IRT, however when the number of learners is relatively small (1, 2.5, and 5 percent of the total batch), IRT cannot evaluate learners’ abilities or forecast their performance in question- solving. In addition, the area under curve (AUC) values from the IRT model declined gradually until 50% of t

In addition, different edges 𝐸𝑖 usually occur at different time nodes, so the lag time (LT), and answer time (RT) in the interaction sequence should be considered as properties of the edges. The input sequence  = {𝑥1,𝑥 2, …,𝑥𝑡 } are sampled and aggregated with higher-order information by graph BG to form the embedding ̂𝑋 = {̂ 𝑥1, ̂ 𝑥2, …, ̂ 𝑥𝑡 }, and the final prediction 𝑟𝑠 𝑖 ∈ {0 , 1} is obtained by the prediction module, which described in Section 4 . 4. The HHSKT model 4.1. Model overview The architecture of our proposed method is shown in Fig. 2. HHSKT consists of two main parts: the question characterization system and

--- p.6 chars=7270 ---
Expert Systems With Applications 215 (2023) 119334 6 Q. Ni et al. Fig. 3. The GNN-based base feature extractor (BFE) is used to extract the interaction features and knowledge structure features in the heterogeneous graph. The update process of the question node embedding is shown in the figure, where the blue part and non-shaded symbols indicate the target and neighboring nodes. the sequence processing system. The details of these processes are explained below. Here, the question characterization consists of two modules, the bottom one is the base feature extractor (BFE), and the other is the hierarchical feature extraction module (HFE). The sequence processing module is the main module for extracting sequence features, which ulti- mately predicts learners’ performance based on their learning behavior and the item difficulty evaluator (DE) combined with the representation information from the BFE and the HFE. Moreover, the essential step of our model is to generate the basic graph (BG) from the exercise, where the basic graph is the heterogeneous graph 𝐵𝐺 = (𝑉,𝐸,𝜆 ). 𝑉 is an adjacency matrix containing three types of nodes: question nodes, knowledge nodes, and learner nodes, the connections of these nodes are saved as 𝐸, and 𝜆 represents node features and edge features. The original input sequence  = {𝑥1,𝑥 2, …,𝑥𝑡 }, each 𝑥𝑖 includ- ing learner 𝑢𝑖, question 𝑞𝑖 and skill 𝑠𝑖, the input sequence is first transformed from each input token 𝑥𝑖 into an embedding ̂ 𝑥𝑖 using an embedding layer (omitted in the figure), where 𝐄𝑠 ∈ R||×𝑑, 𝐄𝑞 ∈ R||×𝑑, 𝐄𝑎 ∈ R2×𝑑. The original input sequence is also sampled to form a Sampled Heterogeneous Graph, after which a higher-order representation of the input sequence is obtained by BFE aggregation ̂𝑋 = {̂ 𝑥1, ̂ 𝑥2, …, ̂ 𝑥𝑡 }, but not directly using the graph hidden states for prediction. An additional transformer layer is used to fuse the difficulty of the difficulty evaluator output ̂𝐷 = {̂𝑑1, ̂𝑑2, …, ̂𝑑𝑡 } and the window attention ̂𝐴𝑇𝑇 = { ̂𝑎𝑡𝑡1, ̂𝑎𝑡𝑡2, …, ̂𝑎𝑡𝑡𝑡 }, the predicted response to the next question𝑞𝑡 is computed from the information in the first𝑡 − 1 elements. The model forms a prediction tensor×× through a fully connected layer, where each element represents the probability that the learner has mastered the corresponding knowledge concept at time 𝑡. 4.2. Base feature extractor Formally, given an input graph 𝐵𝐺 = (𝑉,𝐸,𝜆 ) and a GNN model 𝐺𝑁𝑁𝜃, the key idea of majority graph neural networks (GNNs) is to ag- gregate feature information from a node’s direct (first-order) neighbors, such as inductive representation learning on large graphs (GraphSAGE) or Graph Attention network (GAT) (Hamilton, Ying, & Leskovec, 2017; Veliković et al., 2017). However, they cannot aggregate information from different nodes and are vulnerable to neighbor nodes (Zhang, Song, Huang, Swami, & Chawla, 2019). A basic feature extraction module based on a heterogeneous GNN approach is used for question feature and skill processing, as shown in Fig. 3. The extractor takes the item sequence {𝑞1,𝑞 2, …,𝑞𝑖} and skill sequence {𝑐1,𝑐 2, …,𝑐𝑖} as inputs. Heterogeneous graphical information is used as additional input, learner nodes and interactions between learners, items and re- sponses are included. The output of the feature extractor module is an embedding of items and skills, containing the higher-order features after being crossed. The first contribution based on the intuition is that the differentia- tion of skills and items is not only related to themselves, but also to the interaction sequence. The original graph is heterogeneous, and more information about the features related to the current input sequence can be found on the graph from the input items and skills. So inspired by Heterogeneous Graph Transformer (HGT), the global sampling was introduced in base feature extractor (BFE) which based on multi-hop sampling of small batches of input sequences, to obtain feature data according to richness (Hu, Dong, Wang, & Sun, 2020). After multi-hop sampling, the sampled data and the original data are fed separately into a heterogeneous graph neural network for information aggregation, where different skills and item embeddings contain features of their neighboring related nodes. The raw data use masks to prevent the relationships to be predicted from being captured in advance. 4.2.1. Heterogeneous knowledge extractor The basic extractor is trained and sampled to obtain the relative difficulty information b

Expert Systems With Applications 215 (2023) 119334 7 Q. Ni et al. Fig. 4. Hierarchical feature extractors (HFE) are formed by heterogeneous graph node coarsening operations. The hierarchical feature extractor is formed by a heterogeneous graph node coarsening operation. Node features are extracted by HGT operator, after that nodes are selected and ranked according to the importance of major node features. Besides, the heterogeneous graph structure features are obtained by connecting different hierarchical features through residuals and normalizing them. 4.2.2. Interaction message passing Message passing is the key operation of GNN, which aims to pass information from the source node to the target node. The meta- relationships of edges are added during message passing to mitigate the differences in the distribution of different types of nodes and edges. Messaging of learner interaction data is presented in Eqs. (4) and (5). The 𝑀𝑒𝑠𝑠𝑎𝑔𝑒 (𝑠,𝑒,𝑡 ) corresponding to each node is obtained by merging all message heads. 4.2.3. Message aggregation After completing the computation of attention and messages, the new messages need to be aggregated to the target node to complete the whole update process. The new node features are formed by joining the residuals of the original features with the new features, as Eqs. The new node features contain all attention weights and messages, and the feature information of the target node is updated through residual connections. 4.3. Hierarchical feature extractor Our second intuition is that the skill structure information provided in the dataset does not fully represent the true level of skill differenti- ation. A hierarchical feature extractor is used to obtain structured and hierarchical input features, as shown in Fig. 4. 4.3.1. Heterogeneous coarsening In the HGKT model, hierarchical information has been shown to be crucial for knowledge tracing text (Tong, Wang et al., 2020). How- ever, the hierarchical information obtained by clustering does not fully reflect the real question hierarchical information. Accurate hierarchi- cal features are obtained by heterogeneous graph coarsening using a heterogeneous graph as input. Specifically, the coarsening information of different types of nodes is extended to heterogeneous graphs by computing them to obtain a rich representation of hierarchical features. Between learners and skills are related not only through node infor- mation, but also through graph structure information. To utilize node features and subgraph features, graph convolution is used to compute a vector𝛼 ∈ R𝑛×1 that weights all nodes. Eqs. (8) and (9) demonstrates the node-based coarsening process. 𝛼 = sigmoid ( ̂𝐴𝑋𝑊𝛼 ) (8) 𝑆 =𝓁1 −𝑁𝑜𝑟𝑚 [ ̂𝐴𝑠⊙𝛼 𝑇 𝑠 ] (9) 𝑋 ∈ R𝑛×𝑑 is the node feature matrix and 𝑊𝛼 ∈ R𝑑×1 is the weight vector. In order to coarsen to 𝑚 nodes, the first 𝑚 values of 𝛼 are selected and listed in sorted order. Denote such a vector by 𝛼𝑠 ∈ R𝑚×1, where the subscript 𝑠 denotes sorted. ̂𝐴𝑠 ∈ R𝑛×𝑚 denotes the selected and sorted version of ̂𝐴. 𝓁1 − 𝑁𝑜𝑟𝑚 indicates that 𝑆 is obtained by row-wise normalization. The final coarsening skill node feature representation is shown in Eq. (10). 𝑍 = GNN(𝐴,𝑋 ),𝑍𝑐 =𝑆𝑇𝑍,𝑋𝑐 = GNN(𝐴𝑐,𝑍𝑐 ) (10) where 𝑆 denotes the coarsening matrix. 𝑍 denotes the intermediate hidden vector of the graph coarsening process. 𝑋𝑐 denotes the final graph signal obtained after graph coarsening. 4.4. Sequence feature modeling The processing of serialized data by the model is based on the Transformer model. Since the original dot product attention is not consistent with human short-term memory ability and cannot be fully applied to the learner ability retrospectively, HHSKT has improved the calculation of attention by windowing. 4.4.1. Model training To evaluate the difference between the final prediction of the model and the dataset labels, the negative log likelihood of the observed series was used as the loss function.  = − ∑ 𝑖∈𝐼 (𝑟𝑖 log(𝑝𝑖 ) +(1 −𝑟𝑖 ) log(1 −𝑝𝑖 )) (15) Specifically, at each epoch of the training process, the training loss of the model is updated by minimizing the cross-loss entropy of the predicted value 𝑝𝑖 and the true value 𝑟𝑖, and AdamW is used as an optimization function in the model. 5. Experiments In this section, three open source datasets are used to conduct extensive experiments to demonstrate the effectiveness of the HHSKT model in terms of performance metrics and visualization. 5.1. Implementation details For accurate evaluation of the model, 5-fold cross-validation was used in the experiments, where 20% within each fold was used for testing, 20% for validation, and 60% for training. Experiments were set to stop early to prevent overfitting of the model. The original code and hyperparameters were set for all baselines. In this experiment, the area under the ROC curve (AUC) and the accuracy of prediction (ACC) are used as the evaluation metrics, and the larger the AUC and ACC, the better the prediction performance of the model. Since the length of learners’ time series varies significantly among different data sets, the response series are uniformly intercepted to a length of no more than 200 for the efficiency of the model. Parts longer than the specified length will be truncated to rearrange into a new set of data. The learning rate of our model is set to 0.001, and the batch size for training datasets is set to 128. We use AdamW to optimize our training process. The limitation of the early stopping module is ten epochs. All tests are carried out on the NVIDIA Tesla V100S, which serves as the model’s training platform. Table 3 Datasets. Dataset Student Question Skill Interaction ASSIST2009 3.7k 16k 123 260k ASSIST2017 7k 3k 101 942k Junyi15 24k 835 41 25,797k 5.1.1. Datasets We use three open-source datasets and seven KT models as baselines for our experiments, which are described below. • ASSISTments2009 The ASSISTments2009 dataset is an open source dataset provided by the ASSISTment platform and is also the benchmark dataset for most knowledge tracing models (Feng, Heffernan, & Koedinger, 2009). In our experiment, this dataset contains 258,149 records and 123 KCs. • Junyi15 This dataset comes from Junyi Academy, an E-Learning platform based on Khan Academy (Chang, Hsu, & Chen, 2015). There are 247,605 learners, 835 questions and 41 skills. • ASSISTments2017 This competition uses data from a longitu- dinal study, now over a decade long, led by Professor Ryan Baker and Professor Neil Heffernan (Patikorn, Baker, & Heffernan, 2020). We use 942k records of 7k learners and 3k questions with 101 skills in our experiment. Records of learners with less than 5 interactions were excluded from the data preprocessing. The statistical information of all datasets used in this paper is shown in the Table 3. 5.1.2. Baselines The model is compared with several models to demonstrate the advancedness of the model proposed in this paper. • IRT: IRT (Item Response Theory) (Su et al., 2021) is a paradigm for the design, analysis, and scoring of tests, questionnaires, and similar instruments measuring abilities, attitudes, or other vari- ables. It is considered to be effective in estimating the diffi

--- p.9 chars=7068 ---
Expert Systems With Applications 215 (2023) 119334 9 Q. Ni et al. • GIKT: GIKT proposes a GNN-based knowledge tracing interaction model to address the limitations of data sparsity and multi-skill problems. More specifically, GIKT uses graph convolutional net- works (GCN) to merge the relevance of problems and skills (Yang et al., 2020). • PEBG-DKT: PEBG demonstrates that significant gains in KT can be achieved by pre-training embeddings for each problem on rich auxiliary features (Liu et al., 2020). • FBKT: FBKT proposes a fuzzy Bayesian KT model to cope with continuous scoring scenarios to extend the applicability of the KT model (Liu et al., 2022). Since the output of knowledge tracing models is usually binary val- ues, this paper uses Area Under Curve (AUC) as the primary evaluation metric for all experiments of the KT task, and Root Mean Square Error (RMSE) and F1 are introduced as secondary metrics. 5.2. Experimental results In this section, the HHSKT results are compared with the benchmark KT, and all experimental results are listed in Table 4. The model outperforms the other models on all three datasets, except for the Assist2017 dataset, where it obtains a slightly smaller AUC than the DKT model. In the Assist2009 dataset, the HHSKT model got the best average test AUC of 81.60%. We found that the model’s prediction results were more accurate when the number of exercises per seg- ment was lower. Experimental results for DKT and AKT showed that the attention mechanism accurately captured sequence dependencies. 78.30% and 81.5% were achieved for AKT and PEBG-DKT, respectively, indicating that the graph structure was more effective for multi-skill problems. The AUC of DAS3H is 3% higher than that of PFA, indicating that the forgetting effect of learners is a vital retrospective component of learners’ ability. In Assistment2017, DKT and HHSKT achieved similar results. By contrast, the AUCs of DKT and GIKT are slightly lower than the HHSKT model at 83.30% and 83.80%, respectively. The results suggest that graph convolution is more suitable for this scenario. This may be due to a greater number of scaffolding issues in the Assistment2017 dataset, which limits the distance dependence of the original attention mechanism. In addition, we found that the performance of PFA was about 3% worse than IRT. The results suggest that sometimes when the learner interaction data is enormous (many learner interactions in Assistment2017 exceeded 1000), the volatility of correct and incorrect responses becomes insensitive, while information such as items can still provide accurate fit information. On Junyi15, HHSKT still achieved the best performance on the junyi15 dataset with an AUC of 82.60%. However, the difference with AKT was not significant, probably due to the relatively small number of knowledge points in the Junyi15 dataset. In particular, FBKT achieves similar results to PFA on this dataset, which indicates the effectiveness of FBKT. However, FBKT is not suitable for use as an online knowledge tracing method. In this task, graph neural network-based approaches outperformed regression-based models, such as GIKT and PEBG-DKT, by 10% on average in terms of AUC performance. 5.3. Five variants of the model were obtained by masking some modules in the model. All results were obtained by comparing the two datasets as shown in Table 5. HHSKT consists of multiple modules. Analysis and visualization In this section, the difficulty assessment structure and the base feature extraction structure are visualized to understand the model further. Batches of inputs from the training process of the ASSIST2009 dataset were selected, their overall processing flow was tracked, and their processing was visualized. The weighted heat maps of each of the three attention mechanisms are shown in Fig. 6 . Only attention- weighted information for the first 15 interactions was selected for observation purposes. The original attention weights, AKT attention weights, and HHSKT attention weights are shown from left to right in Fig. 6. The monotonic attention mechanism is significantly shorter than the original attention weights, and the most recent interaction is given a higher weight than the other interactions. This can be attributed to the forgetting behavior of the learning process, where only the most recent interaction can inform the learner’s knowledge state. Furthermore, comparing sequen- tial and monotonic attention reveals that sequential attention places more emphasis on the relevance of the interaction between the current location of the current window and nearby locations and prevents out- of-window attention to information. It indicates that learners are more inclined to be influenced by learning interactions from the most recent period. In addition, Graph embedding information has been shown to be effective in ablation experiments to improve the predictive power of the model. Items embedded in a batch of ASSIST2009 dataset were visualized using t-SNE, and items belonging to the same skill were labeled with the same color, as shown in Fig. 7 . Different colors in Fig. 7(a) represent different aggregation categories, and compared with (b), it can be found that the abstract information represented by different aggregation regions has significantly different meanings, which confirms the effectiveness of our hierarchical feature extractor. The Fig. 7(b) shows the distribution relationship between different items after information aggregation, and it is obvious from the figure that multiple aggregation zones of different sizes are formed, which indicates that graph aggregation well distinguishes different items and assigns unique feature embedding information to them. Fig. 8 shows the parameter scores given by the model after difficulty estimation for the ten questions belonging to the two concepts, respec- tively. The more significant overlap part in the figure represents the more similar items. The significant difference in the distributions of the two skills is evident in Fig. 8(b), which implies the need to differentiate the items’ difficulty differences further when predicting the probability of mastery of items belonging to the two skills. The explanation derived in Fig. 8(a) is that there are still nuanced differences in difficulty for items attributed to the same skill. In addition, the item distribution also shows better consistency compared to the skill distribution. 6. Conclusion In this study, we provide a relational representation of heteroge- neous graphs for the KT problem in order to enhance the representation of input information and strengthen the contextual representation of input features. We additionally transform a matching-model-based dif- ficulty estimate system in order to further discriminate between items by assessing the problem’s difficulty. In addition, we simulate the forgetting behavior of learners using a positional decay kernel function and use an attention strategy based on sequential positional features. We proved the effectiveness of our suggested methodology by applying it to three real-world datasets. Experiments on three real-world datasets demonstrate that our model delivers state-of-the-art performance, with an average AUC gain of 1% to 3% over the baseline.

In addition, we intend to include information such as attempts and work- outs in the graphical feature representation. Lastly, learner cognition while learning is progressive, and we also propose to trace learners’ competency on different questions by tracing their cognitive changes from the perspective of the learners.
