Case study (RQ7) The LGS-KT model’s ability to accurately capture the programming skills contained in student-submitted code, and the accuracy and in- terpretability of the information learned by the model, have been demonstrated in the case diagram 7. We analyzed four cases from two randomly selected students from the AIZU_Python dataset. Firstly, by clustering the programming skills of these two students using t- SNE (Hinton & Van Der Maaten, 2008), we were able to effectively differentiate the skill information between students. Secondly, in case 1 corresponding to student S1, we found that four submitted codes were aggregated into the same category, and their relationships were relatively close. For codes 2 and 3, although they were logically similar, differing only in the check for even or odd numbers, they were recog- nized by the model as the same category due to the use of essentially the same programming grammar. This demonstrates the model’s ability to recognize the same grammatical skills in the same student. However, the problem solved by code 1 was different from that of codes 2 and 3, but they were identified as the same category possibly because they used a similar programming grammar format, which also indicates good generalization ability of the model. However, for code 4, although its programming problem belonged to the same category as codes 2 and 3, it was considered to have the same skills because it did not pass the system check, suggesting that further work is needed to more finely distinguish programming skills. For case 2 corresponding to student S2, codes 5 and 6, from a grammatical skills perspective, these two pieces of code used different syntaxes. Further observation from a logical skill perspective revealed that these two pieces of code actually solved the same problem, only that code 6 was slightly more efficient than code 5, hence the model considered their programming skills to have a high degree of correla- tion. This further proves the effectiveness of our proposed Reweighted Logical Skill Evolution Graph. Additionally, student S1’s case 3 and student S2’s case 4 were classified as the same type of programming ability. By studying the code submitted by the students, we found that these two cases solved the same problem, just using different loop methods to solve it, and the solutions were essentially the same. This finding gives us great confi- dence in the generalization ability of our model, in other words, our model can clearly aggregate different students with similar program- ming abilities and possesses strong grammatical similarity recognition capabilities. This further proves the rationality and high interpretability of our model design. 6. Conclusions and limitations In this study, we initially collected and processed datasets from four different programming languages on the AIZU platform. Recognizing the distinct nature of programming exercises, we introduced the logical and grammatical skills knowledge tracking model (LGS-KT), which separates students’ programming skills into logical and grammatical components for individual modeling. We ca

--- p.12 chars=9291 ---
Neural Networks 185 (2025) 107164 12 X. Sun et al. more accurate assessment of students’ programming skills. Addition- ally, this study does not delve into advanced mechanisms for grammar and code quality scoring. The model’s performance might be influ- enced by student backgrounds and teaching environments, affecting its reliability. The choice of datasets is another limitation, as it only includes a few popular programming languages, potentially limiting the model’s generalizability. Future research should explore diverse datasets, cross-dataset validation, and contextual factors to enhance the model’s robustness. ===== Multi-Granularity Ensemble Interaction Graph Modeling for Knowledge Tracing.pdf pages=13 =====

Contents lists available at ScienceDirect Knowledge-Based Systems journal homepage: www.elsevier.com/locate/knosys Multi-Granularity Ensemble Interaction Graph Modeling for Knowledge Tracing Jing Wang a, Huifang Ma a ,∗, Mengyuan Zhang a, Lei Zhang b, Liang Chang c a College of Computer Science and Engineering, Northwest Normal University, Lanzhou, Gansu 730070, China b College of Information Technology and Communication, Hexi University, Zhangye, Gansu 730040, China c School of Computer Science and Information Security, Guilin University of Electronic Technology, Guilin, Guangxi 541004, China A R T I C L E I N F O Keywords: Intelligent education Knowledge tracing Correctness prediction Graph Neural Networks A B S T R A C T Knowledge tracing (KT) is a crucial educational tool that models students’ mastery of various knowledge concepts by analyzing their historical learning records. Mainstream studies have turned to Deep Neural Networks (DNNs) to effectively trace students’ knowledge states. Nonetheless, these methods may encounter performance barriers due to two critical constraints: the treatment of learning records as singular time series data and the absence of accounting for interaction correlations. To overcome the limitations, we introduce a novel KT model, named Multi-Granularity Ensemble Interaction Graph Modeling for Knowledge Tracing (MGIGKT). Our model incorporates two modules designed to model students’ knowledge states from the perspectives of temporal perception and interaction dependency, respectively. This approach allows for a more comprehensive understanding of students’ learning dynamics and the correlations between their interactions. We have conducted extensive experiments on four datasets to evaluate the superiority and effectiveness of our proposed MGIGKT model. The results of these experiments provide empirical evidence of the model’s enhanced ability to trace students’ knowledge states accurately. 1. Introduction In recent years, the demand for intelligent educational services among students has surged [1–3]. Consequently, online education sys- tems have become increasingly vital in addressing this demand. One such critical component that has come to the forefront is KT, which provides students with immediate and precise feedback. KT entails examining students’ historical learning records to evaluate their com- petency in different knowledge areas, with the goal of forecasting their academic performance ahead. This not only allows for the delivery of varied learning resources but also permits the tailoring of individual- ized learning trajectories to suit personal educational needs. Moreover, KT underpins adaptive education, streamlining the personalization of educational content and its complexity in accordance with students’ learning advancements and knowledge levels. This encompasses fea- tures such as automated test creation and post-lesson tasks, all geared towards a more focused and impactful learning journey. By harnessing the power of KT through the analysis of students’ historical learning records, online education systems can acquire in-depth insights into their educational states, significantly enhancing the online learning experience for both students and educators. ∗ Corresponding author. E-mail addresses: 2022222231@nwnu.edu.cn (J. Wang), mahuifang@yeah.net (H. Ma), 2023222106@nwnu.edu.cn (M. Zhang), zhanglei@hxu.edu.cn (L. Zhang), changl@guet.edu.cn (L. Chang). Currently, numerous studies have been dedicated to exploring the task of KT [4–7]. Due to the powerful feature extraction capabilities of DNNs for modeling complex cognitive processes, recent researches have predominantly relied on more complex and accurate DNNs ar- chitectures. These methods can be broadly categorized into two types: conventional KT methods and graph-based KT methods. The first cat- egory of conventional KT methods models students’ knowledge states from a temporal perspective using deep learning techniques, aiming to dynamically capture the evolving proficiency of students. For example, DKT [8] is the first work to apply Recurrent Neural Networks (RNNs) to the KT task. Due to its simple structure and efficient performance, it has been widely adopted as a paradigm by subsequent deep learning KT models. Following this, more types of neural network technologies have been introduced to improve temporal KT models. For example, DKVMN [9] introduces a memory-augmented network that uses mem- ory networks to sto

--- p.3 chars=7488 ---
J. Wang et al. an interaction sequence of a student over six historical timesteps. This sequence includes the exercises the student interacted with and their responses at each timestep. Fig. 2(b), (c), and (d) respectively depict the sequential relationships among knowledge concepts, cases where exercises share the same knowledge concept and the knowledge con- cepts involved have a sequential relationship, and the final interaction correlations calculated using Eq. (9) mentioned in Section 3.2.3. In our example, from the perspective of shared knowledge concepts, although 𝑖1,𝑖2 and𝑖3 all share knowledge concept𝐾 𝐶 1, the correlation between 𝑖1 and 𝑖3 is higher since the student’s responses to 𝑖1 and 𝑖3 are con- sistent, while the response to𝑖2 differs. Similarly, from the perspective of sequential knowledge concepts, interactions𝑖4,𝑖5 and𝑖6 involve the sequential knowledge concepts𝐾 𝐶 5,𝐾 𝐶 6 and𝐾 𝐶 7, further indicating high correlation among these exercises. Notably, since the responses for 𝑖4 and 𝑖6 are consistent, their correlation is higher than that between 𝑖5 and 𝑖6. Therefore, considering both the latent correlations in the student’s historical interactions and their responses on the exercises helps achieve more accurate interaction correlations, which is crucial for enhancing the performance of the KT model. In this paper, we propose a model named MGIGKT, designed to explore students’ learning patterns at multiple granularities and com- prehensively model the latent correlations in student interactions, aim- ing to achieve a more accurate representation of students’ knowledge states. Specifically, on one hand, to model learning patterns at multiple granularities, we introduce a Multi-Scale Sequence Perception Modeling module to encode students’ historical learning records at different time scales. To reduce the computational complexity of encoding, we incor- porate an L2-regularized Transformer to improve efficiency. Through a multi-head attention mechanism, we can obtain a knowledge state that integrates multiple scales. On the other hand, to comprehensively model the latent correlations between interactions, we propose an Interaction Graph Dependency Modeling module, which creates interaction graphs from the perspectives of similarity and dissimilarity. We design a dual-channel encoding mechanism to understand students’ knowledge states from these perspectives. Additionally, we employ cross-channel contrastive learning to optimize the learned knowledge states, ensuring that the knowledge states of the same student remain stable across different perspectives. Finally, the Fusion and Prediction module fully considers the informative knowledge states modeled by the previous two modules to perform accurate and comprehensive KT. To summarize, the main contributions of our work are as follows: • Model learning patterns at multiple granularities: We find that different students exhibit varying learning patterns at differ- ent time granularities. To address this, we propose the Multi-Scale Sequence Perception Modeling module to comprehensively model students’ diverse learning patterns across multiple granularities, resulting in more accurate knowledge states. • Model the latent correlations between interactions: We dis- cover more complex latent correlations among students’ histor- ical interactions. To tackle this, we introduce the Interaction Graph Dependency Modeling module, which constructs interac- tion graphs and utilizes a dual-channel encoding component with a cross-channel contrastive learning mechanism to delve into the underlying correlations in student interactions. • Experimental evaluation results: Extensive experiments con- ducted on four KT datasets validate the effectiveness of MGIGKT in assessing students’ knowledge states. The remainder of this paper is structured as follows: In Section 2, we provide a comprehensive review of related works pertaining to our research problem. Detailed information about the overall pipeline and components of our model is illustrated in Section 3. In Section 4, we analyze the experimental results obtained from our research. Finally, in Section 5, we present the concluding remarks and summarize our work. 2. Related work In this section, we will introduce relevant work on KT categorized into two types: conventional KT methods and graph-based KT methods. 2.1. Conventional KT methods In recent years, with the advancement of deep learning, an in- creasing number of DNNs

Problem statement In the KT task, let  = {𝑠1, 𝑠2, …, 𝑠𝑁 },  = {𝑒1, 𝑒2, …, 𝑒𝑀 }, and  = {𝑘𝑐1, 𝑘𝑐2, …, 𝑘𝑐𝐾 } denote a set of 𝑁 students, a set of 𝑀 exercises and a set of 𝐾 knowledge concepts, respectively. Suppose a student 𝑠’s historical learning records of length 𝑡 is represented as 𝑠 = {(𝑒1, 1, 𝑎1), (𝑒2, 2, 𝑎2), …, (𝑒𝑡, 𝑡, 𝑎𝑡)}. Each interaction is rep- resented by a triple, taking the interaction at timestep𝑡 as an example, where 𝑒𝑡 (𝑒𝑡 ∈ ) denotes the exercise answered by the student at the timestep 𝑡, 𝑡 ⊂  is the subset of knowledge concepts contained in 𝑒𝑡, 𝑎𝑡 ∈ {0, 1} is the correctness of responses, typically, 𝑎𝑡 = 1 if the student answers 𝑒𝑡 correctly, otherwise 𝑎𝑡 = 0. To provide a clear illustration of the model structure, we present a summary of the key mathematical notations in Table 1. These notations will be utilized throughout the paper. Specifically, we use bold uppercase letters to represent matrices, bold lowercase letters to represent vectors, and calligraphy uppercase letters to represent sets. KT Task Definition: Given the learning records 𝑠 of the student 𝑠 up to timestep𝑡, our objective is to evaluate the mastery of the student on various knowledge concepts after answering each exercise. This is achieved by predicting the probability of students correctly answering a new exercise𝑒𝑡+1 at timestep𝑡 + 1, denoted as ̂ 𝑦𝑡+1 =𝑝(𝑎𝑡+1 = 1|𝑒𝑡+1, 𝑠), and to trace students’ knowledge states. 3.2. MGIGKT In this section, we will introduce the overall process of the proposed MGIGKT model and provide detailed explanations of each component. The architecture of the MGIGKT model is illustrated in Fig. 3. The primary aim of MGIGKT is to model the knowledge states of students from both temporal and interaction graph perspectives, representing dynamic and static viewpoints, respectively. To achieve this goal, we design three modules corresponding to the aforementioned modeling processes. These modules are the Multi-Scale Sequential Perception Mod- eling module, Interaction Graph Dependency Modeling module, and Fusion and Prediction module. The Multi-Scale Sequential Perception Modeling module primarily involves granular partitioning and encoding of his- torical learning records to obtain multi-granularity knowledge states of the student over time. The Interaction Graph Dependency Modeling module introduces interaction graph filters, which use low-pass and high-pass filtering based on similar and dissimilar interaction graphs, respectively, to derive the student’s static knowledge state. Finally, the Fusion and Prediction module integrates the dynamic and static knowledge states. We utilize the final knowledge state to predict the student’s response in the next timestep. 3.2.1. Embedding layer We first initialize embedding matrices for questions, knowledge concepts, and response results as 𝐐 ∈ R𝑀×𝑑, 𝐊𝐂 ∈ R𝐾×𝑑, and 𝐀 ∈ R2×𝑑, respectively, where 𝑑 represents the embedding dimension. To obtain the representation of the historical learning records of student 𝑠, we look up the aforementioned three matrices to obtain the exercise embedding matrix 𝐄𝑠 and the interaction embedding matrix 𝐈𝑠. 𝐄𝑠 𝑖 = 𝐪𝑖 ⊕ ( 1 𝑚 ∑ 𝑘𝑐𝑘∈𝑖 𝐤𝐜𝑘), (1) 𝐈𝑠 𝑖 = 𝐪𝑖 ⊕ ( 1 𝑚 ∑ 𝑘𝑐𝑘∈𝑖 𝐤𝐜𝑘)⊕ 𝐚𝑖, (2) where ⊕ represents concatenation operation. 1 𝑚 ∑ 𝑘𝑐𝑗 ∈𝑖 𝐤𝐜𝑗 denotes the average pooling of knowledge concepts embeddings contained in question 𝑞𝑖. 3.2.2. Multi-scale sequential perception modeling(MSPM) During the learning process, educational systems, school policies, and students’ learning habits determine the establishment of learning cycles, such as teaching months, semesters, and years, as well as various time periods within a day. As a result, students’ knowledge states exhibit multi-scale patterns of variation over time, reflecting their staged learning progress across different cycles (e.g., months, semesters, and years). To effectively model this c

Fine-grained modeling of students’ knowledge states: Fine- grained modeling of students’ historical learning records allows for more precise tracing of changes in their knowledge states over different learning periods, thereby providing a deeper understanding of their learning characteristics and behavior patterns [28]. To achieve this goal, we design a fine-grained attention network to more effectively capture knowledge state changes at specific granularities. Specifically, we introduce a time-aware encoder to generate knowledge state rep- resentations 𝐇𝑇 𝑀1 and 𝐇𝑇 𝑀2, where 𝑇 𝑀1 and 𝑇 𝑀2 represent the different temporal granularities. Since the steps for modeling knowl- edge states at both granularities are the same, we will omit the subscripts 1 and 2 in the following text and use 𝐇𝑇 𝑀 directly. After obtaining representations capturing short-term knowledge states, a self-attention operation is applied to them to obtain granularity-specific knowledge state representations. In our model, we configure transformer modules with two scales, ultimately generating three granularity-specific knowledge state representations: 𝐇𝐸, 𝐇𝑇 𝑀1, 𝐇𝑇 𝑀2. Finally, we integrate the three granularity-specific knowledge state representations into the same latent space using a multi-scale aggrega- tion layer, as shown below: 𝐇𝑆 =𝑓1 ( 𝐇𝐸 ∥ 𝐇𝑇 𝑀 1 ∥ 𝐇𝑇 𝑀 2 ), (8) where ∥ denotes matrix concatenation, and the function𝑓1 is a projec- tion function that transforms the embedding matrix of dimension 3𝑑 into dimension𝑑. Finally, we obtain the knowledge state matrix 𝐇𝑆 of the student after being modeled by the sequence module. 3.2.3. Interaction graph dependency modeling (IGDM) As previously mentioned, we believe that in real online education systems, there are latent correlations between the knowledge concepts involved in interactions. In addition to knowledge concept informa- tion, interaction data also includes information on whether students answered the exercises correctly. If we group interactions solely based on their association with knowledge concepts, we might overlook the correctness of the student’s responses, leading to the loss of important answer outcome information [29]. To more accurately describe the complex relationships in students’ historical interactions, we introduce an Interaction Graph Dependency Modeling module that can describe the relationships between interactions. Specifically, we construct two types of interaction graphs: one to represent the similarities between interactions and the other to describe their dissimilarities. Through these two graphs, we cannot only capture the direct correlations in students’ historical interactions but also reveal the latent, deeper corre- lations. We apply Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) to these two graphs, performing low-pass and high-pass filtering operations. Specifically, we use the dissimilarity matrix to push apart the representations of dis- similar nodes, reducing interference from dissimilar neighbors, and the similarity matrix to bring the representations of similar nodes closer. This approach allows us to obtain distinctive representations of stu- dents’ knowledge states. We further optimize these representations by employing a contrastive learning mechanism. This mechanism provides additional supervisory signals to the model by comparing knowledge state representations across different interaction graphs, guiding the model to generate more consistent and meaningful representations. Consequently, the model can predict students’ future learning per- formance with greater accuracy, as it 

--- p.6 chars=6783 ---
J. Wang et al. 𝐀(𝑠𝑚) =𝑠𝑖𝑔 𝑚𝑜𝑖𝑑(𝐈⊤𝐈). (10) Next, based on the obtained similarity matrix 𝐀(𝑠𝑚), we construct the adjacency weight matrix 𝐀(𝑛𝑠) for the dissimilarity interaction graph. 𝐀(𝑛𝑠) = 𝐉 − 𝐀(𝑠𝑚), (11) where 𝐉 represents a matrix where all elements are 1. Dual-Channel Encoding: To comprehensively model the latent cor- relations between interactions and learn the static knowledge state of students from both similar and dissimilar interaction graphs, we design two encoders that perform low-pass and high-pass filtering on these two different views, respectively. This approach allows us to capture shared information among interactions based on similar edges, while filtering out irrelevant information between adjacent interactions based on dissimilar edges. Such a processing approach aids in more accurately identifying and leveraging the key features within interaction data, thereby enhancing the accuracy and efficiency of KT. Using a low-pass graph filter can smooth features along the nodes [30], as low-pass filtering specifically captures the low-frequency in- formation in the graph signal, thereby preserving the shared knowl- edge between similar nodes. Consequently, in the similarity interaction graph, we choose to use a simple low-pass GNN as the low-pass graph filter. This is represented as: 𝐈(𝑠𝑚) 0 =𝑀 𝐿𝑃1(𝐈), (12) 𝐈(𝑠𝑚) 𝑙 = 𝐀(𝑠𝑚) ⋅ 𝐈(𝑠𝑚) 𝑙−1 ⋅ 𝐖(𝑠𝑚) 𝑙−1, (13) where 𝐖(𝑠𝑚) 𝑙−1 ∈ R𝑑×𝑑 is a learnable projection matrix. Dissimilarity interaction graphs consist of edges that connect dis- similar nodes. In this view, applying a low-pass filter might smooth out the features of connected nodes, thus blurring the differences between dissimilar nodes and reducing node discriminability. To avoid this, we employ a high-pass graph filter (the inverse operation of a low-pass filter) to sharpen the features of nodes along the edges. The purpose of this is to distinguish between nodes that are dif- ferent yet interconnected. The process of graph convolution on the dissimilarity interaction graph is consistent with that on the similarity interaction graph. The primary difference lies in replacing the adja- cency weight matrix in the convolution operation with the dissimilarity adjacency weight matrix 𝐀(𝑛𝑠) and different𝑀 𝐿𝑃2. In this way, we ulti- mately obtain the knowledge state 𝐈(𝑛𝑠) modeled based on the student’s dissimilarity interaction graph. By utilizing two dedicated encoders, we obtain two distinct sets of representations, each capturing low-frequency and high-frequency in- formation, respectively. These representations reveal the characteristics of a student’s knowledge state from two complementary perspectives. To integrate the information from these viewpoints, we concatenate the representations, thereby forming the final static representation of the student’s knowledge state: 𝐇𝐺 =𝑓2(𝐈(𝑠𝑚) ∥ 𝐈(𝑛𝑠)), (14) where𝑓2 is a projection function that transforms the embedding matrix of dimension 2𝑑 into dimension 𝑑. 3.2.4. Fusion and prediction (FP) Ultimately, we successfully construct two models of knowledge state: one, denoted as 𝐇𝑆, which models learning patterns from multi- ple granularities, and the other, denoted as 𝐇𝐺, which comprehensively simulates the latent correlations between interactions. To integrate these two perspectives of knowledge state, we employ an adaptive aggregation strategy. The aggregation process is as follows: 𝛼𝑆 𝑡 = 𝜶⊤ ⋅ 𝐖𝛼 ⋅ 𝐇𝑆 𝑡 𝜶⊤ ⋅ 𝐖𝛼 ⋅ 𝐇𝑆 𝑡 + 𝜶⊤ ⋅ 𝐖𝛼 ⋅ 𝐇𝐺 𝑡 , (15) 𝛼𝐺 𝑡 = 𝜶⊤ ⋅ 𝐖𝛼 ⋅ 𝐇𝐺 𝑡 𝜶⊤ ⋅ 𝐖𝛼 ⋅ 𝐇𝑆 𝑡 + 𝜶⊤ ⋅ 𝐖𝛼 ⋅ 𝐇𝐺 𝑡 , (16) 𝐡𝐭 =𝛼𝑆 𝑡 𝐇𝑆 𝑡 ⊕ 𝛼𝐺 𝑡 𝐇𝐺 𝑡 , (17) ̂ 𝑦𝑡+1 =𝑀 𝐿𝑃 (𝐡𝑡⊕ 𝐄𝑡+1), (18) where 𝜶 ∈ R𝑑 is learnable projection vectors, 𝐖𝛼 ∈ R𝑑×𝑑 is learnable projection matrices. 𝛼𝑆 𝑡 , 𝛼𝐺 𝑡 represent the importance of 𝐇𝑆 𝑡 and 𝐇𝐺 𝑡 at time 𝑡, used for the final aggregation of knowledge state. Model Training: To train all the parameters in the model, we leverage the binary cross-entropy loss as a loss function between the predicted performance ̂ 𝑦𝑡+1 and actual performance𝑦𝑡+1. The following objective function is defined over training data: 𝐿𝑝 = − ∑ 𝑡+1 (𝑦𝑡+1𝑙 𝑜𝑔 ̂ 𝑦𝑡+1 + (1 − 𝑦𝑡+1 )𝑙 𝑜𝑔(1 − ̂ 𝑦𝑡+1 )). (19) To enhance the effectiveness of learning student knowledge state representations from two distinct graph structures, we incorporate the contrastive learning. This mechanism provides additional supervi- sory signals to the model by comparing the representations generated from these two different graph structures. This approach encourages the model to generate consistent semantic representation

Novelty analysis Our method, MGIGKT, captures the multi-granularity learning pat- terns present in students’ historical learning records and the latent correlations between interactions, offering a nuanced representation of students’ knowledge states. This model revolves around two pri- mary modeling processes: firstly, it engages in multi-scale sequential perception modeling on students’ historical learning records, which showcase diverse temporal distribution characteristics across multiple time scales; secondly, it establishes two varieties of interaction graphs based on distinct response outcomes, facilitating interaction graph dependency modeling. Our work excels in the following pivotal aspects: (1) Recognizing the diverse learning patterns exhibited by different students across varying temporal granularities, we have devised a methodology to capture these patterns through multiple time scales. This led to the development of a Multi-Scale Sequence Perception Modeling module aimed at simulating students’ learning behaviors comprehensively at different granularities, thereby offering a more precise portrayal of their knowledge states. While recent studies like QKT [31] have focused on segmenting students’ historical learning records based on quizzes, relying solely on a single granularity over- looks the intricacies inherent in the learning journey. In contrast, our approach delves into modeling students’ knowledge states across diverse time granularities, promising an improved accuracy in temporal knowledge state representation. (2) We introduce a pioneering Interaction Graph Dependency Mod- eling module that intricately captures the latent correlations existing between interactions. Traditional GCN-based approaches have primar- ily concentrated on relationships among students, interactions, and knowledge concepts. For example, SGKT [32] and HGKT [33] devise various graph structures to tackle knowledge tracing tasks. Never- theless, these structures often fail to unveil the deeper interconnec- tions within historical interactions, such as shared knowledge concepts across problems, and tend to overlook the significance of response correctness in interactions. In contrast, our method constructs inter- action graphs based on both correct and incorrect responses, aiming to provide a more granular depiction of the interaction dynamics. It is worth noting that recent advances in node representation learning have validated the efficacy of modeling nodes from perspectives of similarity and dissimilarity. The GREET model [34] leverages dual- channel encoding to learn node representations via homophilic and heterophilic views, bolstered by an edge discriminator during training. Drawing inspiration from GREET, our approach not only introduces the construction of similarity and dissimilarity graphs for knowledge trac- ing tasks but also demonstrates utility for broader graph representation learning endeavors. 4. Baselines To evaluate the performance, we compare MGIGKT with two groups of methods, i.e., Conventional KT methods and Graph-based KT meth- ods. For each group, we select representative baselines and briefly introduce them below. (1) Conventional KT methods • DKT [8] introduces RNNs into the KT task for the first time, employing LSTM to trace the dynamic changes of students’ knowl- edge states during the learning process. • DKVMN [9] incorporates memory networks into the KT task, utilizing a static key matrix to store knowledge concepts and a dynamic value matrix to store and update students’ knowledge states. • SAKT [10] employs a self-attention mechanism to model students’ knowledge states, considering the correlation between predicting exercises and past interactions to model students’ knowledge states. Meanwhile, it can also address data sparsity issues. • MRTKT [19] utilizes the Transformer architecture to model the various interaction relationships present in students’ historical learning records, finely modeling the students’ knowledge states. • DTransformer [36] not only designs a proficiency modeling mod- ule from the exercise level to the knowledge concept level but also incorporates a contrastive learning mechanism to model the stability of knowledge states during the KT process. (2) Graph-based KT methods • GKT [11] constructs a graph structure where the nodes represent students, exercises, and knowledge concepts, and the edges repre- sent the interactions between them. It uses GNNs to capture these interaction patterns and infer the students’ knowledge states. • DGEKT [12] establishes hypergraphs based on knowledge con- cepts and constructs directed transition graphs according to stu- dents’ learning sequence time information. It then employs a knowledge distillation framework to integrate the modeling of student knowledge states based on the two types of graphs. 4.3. Comparison with the efficiency of various baselines(RQ2) To address RQ2, this section presents a comparative analysis of our model’s runtime and performance against five state-of-the-art knowl- edge tracing methods (namely, DKT, DKVMN, SAKT, DTransformer, and GKT). All models are evaluated under identical hardware con- ditions. Fig. 5 illustrates a scatter plot of experimental results on two knowledge tracing datasets of different scales, where the 𝑦-axis represents model AUC and the 𝑥-axis represents runtime per epoch. Models closer to the upper left corner exhibit superior performance. As shown in Fig. 5, our proposed method consistently achieves either the best or second-best performance across both datasets, while maintaining an acceptable runtime, outperforming other baseline mod- els. From a performance perspective, the superiority of our approach is mainly attributable to the inclusion of two core modules: a multi- scale sequence perception modeling module and an interaction graph dependency modeling module. These modules perform in-depth model- ing of students’ knowledge states from dynamic and static perspectives, respectively. On one hand, this design captures students’ learning pat- terns at multiple time granularities more comprehensively; on the other hand, it models the underlying dependencies between interactions, significantly enhancing the model’s accuracy in the knowledge tracing task. In terms of efficiency, although our method involves higher com- putational complexity due to the use of multiple Transformer modules, resulting in a mid-range runtime, we mitigate the high time com- plexity of Transformers in modeling overall student knowledge states by introducing an L2 regularization strategy. Despite requiring more computation time than the fastest method, DKT, our model’s substan- tial performance improvement keeps its runtime within an acceptable range. 4.6. Ablation study(RQ3) In this subsection, to justify the rationality of key components and contrastive learning loss in MGIGKT, we conduct experiments on several variants of MGIGKT and the results are shown in Fig. 6. Effect of Multi-Scale Sequential Perception Modeling module. To investigate whether the Multi-Scale Sequential Perception Modeling (MSPM) module can effectively model students’ learning patterns at multiple granularities, we design three variants: MGIGKT(w/o)-MSPM, MGIGKT(w/o)-CM, and MGIGKT(w/o)-FM. The first variant completely removes the MSPM module, the second variant removes the coarse- grained modeling of students’ knowledge states using L2 regularization, and the third variant removes the fine-grained modeling of students’ knowledge states. By analyzing the results in Fig. 6(a), we observe that compared to our MGIGKT model, these three variants show vary- ing degrees of performance decline on the ASSIST2017 dataset. This validates two key findings: (1) The MSPM module can effectively model students’ learning patterns at multiple granularities and capture sufficient key feature information; (2) There is a mutually support- ive and complementary relationship between the coarse-grained and fine-grained modeling modules, which is crucial for comprehensively modeling students’ knowledge states and improving the performance of KT models. Effect of Interaction Graph Dependency Modeling module. To validate the effectiveness of the Interaction Graph Dependency Model- ing (IGDM) module, we introduce three variants: MGIGKT(w/o)-IGDM, MGIGKT(w/o)-SM, and MGIGKT(w/o)-NS. These variants represent the removal of the IGDM module, the similarity interaction graph model- ing module, and the dissimilarity interaction graph modeling module from the original model, respectively. The results in Fig. 6(b) indicate that these three variants show performance declines across all four datasets. This finding strongly supports the importance of the IGDM module, which is specifically designed to comprehensively capture the latent correlations present in students’ historical interaction se- quences. The IGDM module is crucial for more accurately predicting changes in students’ knowledge states and enhancing the performance of Knowledge Tracing (KT) models. Further analysis reveals that remov- ing either the similarity or dissimilarity interaction graph affects model performance. This is because the similarity and dissimilarity graphs capture the similarities and differe

--- p.10 chars=5759 ---
J. Wang et al. Fig. 6. Performance comparison between our method and variants. considers both similarities and dissimilarities between interactions. This underscores the effectiveness and necessity of our approach. In summary, retaining the interaction graph modules is essential for constructing an effective knowledge tracing model, as they are crucial for more accurately predicting changes in students’ knowledge states and improving KT model performance. Effect of Contrastive Learning Loss: To investigate the role of con- trastive learning loss in improving the accuracy of predicting students’ knowledge states, we design a variant, MGIGKT(w/o)-CL, where the contrastive learning loss is removed from the total loss. The experimen- tal results, shown in Fig. 6(b), combined with the ablation study results on the IGDM module, reveal that MGIGKT(w/o)-IGDM performs the worst, indicating that completely removing the IGDM module has the most significant impact on model performance. The two variants that retain single-channel encoding still include contrastive learning loss— MGIGKT(w/o)-SM and MGIGKT(w/o)-NS—outperform MGIGKT(w/o)- IGDM. This suggests that even without dual-channel encoding, con- trastive learning loss can still enhance model effectiveness to some extent. Furthermore, the variant MGIGKT(w/o)-CL, which lacks con- trastive learning loss, performs better than the single-channel vari- ants MGIGKT(w/o)-SM and MGIGKT(w/o)-NS, highlighting the criti- cal role of dual-channel encoding. Nevertheless, the complete model that combines dual-channel encoding and contrastive learning loss achieves the best performance. This is because dual-channel encoding can capture shared information between interactions using similar edges and filter out irrelevant information between neighboring in- teractions through dissimilar edges. Meanwhile, contrastive learning loss acts as a constraint mechanism, encouraging the model to gen- erate consistent semantic representations across the two graph struc- tures, thereby enhancing representation quality and overall model performance. 4.7. Hyperparameter analysis(RQ4) In this subsection, we investigate the impact of different settings for several key hyperparameters in MGIGKT, including the embedding dimension 𝑑, the loss weight 𝜆, which is used to control the impact of the contrastive loss on the model, and the threshold w. The results are shown in Fig. 7. Embedding Dimension: The embedding dimension 𝑑 determines the amount of information the model can capture. As shown in Fig. 7(a), we present the hyperparameter analysis results for the em- bedding dimension on the ASSIST2017 dataset. It can be observed that as the dimension gradually increases, the model’s performance initially improves but then levels off. Ultimately, the performance peaks at 𝑑 = 128. Compared to the optimal 𝑑, smaller or larger 𝑑 values lead to a decrease in performance. This is because smaller 𝑑 values cannot capture enough feature information, limiting the model’s expressiveness and performance. On the other hand, larger 𝑑 values may introduce too much noise and redundant information, causing the model to overfit and thus affecting its generalization capability and performance. Contrastive Learning Loss Weight: The loss weight 𝜆 plays a crucial role in determining the importance of contrastive loss on the model’s performance. As shown in Fig. 7(b), we present the hyper- parameter analysis results for the loss weight on the ASSIST2017 dataset. The model’s performance gradually improves with increasing loss weight in the range of 0.00001 to 0.1, peaking at 𝜆 = 0.01. After this point, the performance slightly decreases as the loss weight exceeds this value. This change may be because smaller loss weights fail to sufficiently emphasize the contribution of contrastive loss, reducing the model’s efficiency in learning and distinguishing different features. On the other hand, excessively large loss weights may overly emphasize contrastive loss, causing the model to focus too much on contrastive learning while neglecting the learning of other critical features, neg- atively impacting overall performance. This result indicates that the loss associated with prediction contributes more significantly to model training, while the contrastive loss serves an auxiliary role, helping the model better learn and differentiate features. Case studies(RQ5) To gain a deeper understanding of how the MGIGKT model tracks students’ evolving knowledge states, this section presents a visual anal- ysis of the knowledge tracing process from two different perspectives. Specifically, we select the historical learning records of the student with ID 23 from the ASSIST2009 dataset and illustrate the changes in the student’s knowledge state under two key modules—the Multi- Scale Sequence Perception Modeling module and the Interaction Graph Dependency Modeling module. In the Multi-Scale Sequence Perception Modeling module, we observe the student’s knowledge state changes at different granularities. In the Interaction Graph Dependency Modeling module, we display the constructed interaction graph and the student’s knowledge state at each moment. Case study of modeling learning patterns at multiple granu- larities: To evaluate the KT capability of the MGIGKT model, we employ a multi-granularity analysis approach to visualize changes in students’ knowledge states. Fig. 8 illustrates the learning activities of the student at daily, weekly, and overall granularities, and provides the final knowledge states at different granularities. By observing her daily learning activities, we are able to trace her mastery of knowledge concepts matrix product, matrix transpose, and inequality. For instance, on Monday, her performance in matrix product improved, while on Tuesday, due to mistakes in matrix transpose-related questions, her mastery level decreased. By Wednesday, she answers all questions re- lated to inequality correctly, thus improving her mastery of inequality. Further analysis of the student’s weekly learning activities provides an overall view of her grasp of the three knowledge concepts. In the first week, her accuracy in matrix product and inequality is high, whereas it is low in matrix transpose, indicating a need for more practice in matrix transpose. By the second week, her accuracy in matrix product and matrix transpose have improved, but it decreases in inequality, highlighting her weaknesses in inequality and suggesting a need for more focused practice on this knowledge concept. A comprehensive analysis of all her learning activities gives us an overall view of her performance in the three knowledge concepts. As shown in the figure, her performance in matrix product is the best, her performance in matrix transpose needed improvement, and she has a good grasp of inequality. Case study of comprehensively modeling the latent correlations between interactions: We provide the historical learning records of the student, which include six interactions corresponding to three knowledge concepts with sequential relationships. We present the con- struction of similar and dissimilar interaction graphs from an inter- action graph dependency perspective. It can be observed that when there are sequential relationships between knowledge concepts, the similarity between interactions also increases to a certain extent. When the exercises involve the same knowledge concepts but with different answers, the similarity between interactions decreases, while dissimi- larity increases. As shown in Fig. 9, when a student correctly answers exercises related to the red knowledge concept, the proficiency in the red knowledge concept increases, whereas it decreases when they answer exercises related to the red knowledge concept incorrectly. When correctly answering exercises related to the yellow knowledge concept, the proficiency of the prerequisite knowledge concepts also increases simultaneously. In summary, there are two key observations: • By combining the results from fine-grained, mid-grained, and overall levels of modeling, we can gain a more comprehensive understanding of the student’s learning state and provide person- alized learning suggestions accordingly. • Considering the correlations of interactions between students is significantly beneficial for accurately tracing and understanding their knowledge states, which not only aids the model in deeply uncovering the underlying connections within historical interac- tion data but also effectively captures the dynamic changes in a student’s knowledge state when answering exercises involving potentially related knowledge concepts. 5. Conclusion In this work, we propose a KT model named MGIGKT, which mod- els students’ knowledge states from dynamic, time-based perspectives and static, interaction-graph-based perspectives to predict their fu- ture performance. We first finely partition students’ historical learning records, modeling their knowledge states granularly in the Multi-Scale Sequential Perception Modeling module to explore learning patterns at multiple granularities. Simultaneously, we statically model students’ knowledge states from two constructed interaction views in the In- teraction Graph Dependency Modeling module to capture the latent correlations in student interactions comprehensively. MGIGKT captures different types of contextual information in student learning records, enhancing its modeling capability. Experimental validation on four datasets confirms the effectiveness of our proposed model. Knowledge-Based Systems 315 (2025) 113281 Available online 8 March 2025 0950-7051/© 2025 Elsevier B.V. Contents lists available at ScienceDirect Knowledge-Based Systems journal homepage: www.elsevier.com/locate/knosys Graph knowledge tracing in cognitive situation: Validation of classic assertions in cognitive psychology Qianxi Wu a, Weidong Ji a,b ,∗, Guohui Zhou a,b, Yingchun Yang c a College of Computer Information Engineering, Harbin Normal University, Harbin, 150025, China b Future Teachers Teaching and Intelligent Development Laboratory, Harbin Normal University, Harbin, 150025, China c College of Literature and History and Culture, Qiqihar University, Qiqihar, 161000, China A R T I C L E I N F O Keywords: Knowledge Tracing Cognitive situation Hyper-Graph Neural Network Directed Graph Convolutional Neural Network Cognitive psychology A B S T R A C T Knowledge Tracing (KT) is a fundamental and challenging task in intelligent education, aiming to trace learners’ knowledge states and learning processes, providing better support and guidance for teaching and addressing mental factors. Previous KT tasks have focused on considering learners’ exposure to extrinsic environmental factors while ignoring the influence of intrinsic psychological factors. Moreover, previous methods have adopted a single perspective in modeling learners’ knowledge states, ignoring the diversity of states in the learning process. To address these issues, we define the concept of cognitive situation through the guidance of cognitive psychology theory to help to explain the extrinsic influence and intrinsic cognition of learners within complex learning environments. Moreover, we design a Cognitive Situation-based Graph KT (CSGKT) model to quantify learners’ influences in the cognitive process by modeling schemas capturing intrinsic characteristics and extrinsic factors through Hyper-Graph Neural Networks (HGNN). Second, we utilize a Directed Graph Convolutional Neural Network (DGCNN) to capture the correlation information between knowledge concepts and structure the learner’s cognitive activities and knowledge states, adding a detailed representation of multiple states of the learning process. In addition, we use the Erase-add Gate to filter out the knowledge states that do not match the learner’s current cognitive activities to stabilize the learner’s due cognition. In our experiments, we selected nine baseline models from three mainstream approaches, including sequence-based approaches, Transformer -based approaches, and complex structure-based approaches. The experimental results show that our models outperform these baseline models. At the same time, we also verify two classic assertions in cognitive psychology, namely, the ‘‘short-term memory forgetting of knowledge concepts is mainly caused by interference rather than memory trace fading" and the ‘‘cognitive imagery and perceptual function play an equivalent role in the cognitive process", which further support the feasibility of the model. 1. Introduction The rapid development of artificial intelligence and big data tech- nologies has made intelligent education a key innovation in the ed- ucational field. Technologies [1] such as Knowledge Tracing (KT) provide personalized learning [2,3] support through real-time analysis of learners’ behaviors and knowledge states, aiming to improve learning efficiency and educational quality by optimizing resource allocation. However, a major challenge in achieving intelligent education is accu- rately understanding learners’ cognitive processes in complex learning environments. Learning is a social process embedded in specific con- texts, influenced by external factors and individual characteristics. Ignoring this complexity hinders a comprehensive understanding of learners’ cognitive states and impairs personalized support. ∗ Corresponding author. E-mail address: kingjwd@126.com (W. Ji). Education is inherently a complex system [4], encompassing nu- merous intricate factors such as interactions between individuals and groups, mutual shaping of environment and cognition, and the inte- gration and analysis of multi-source heterogeneous data. A holistic understanding of learning environments necessitates interdisciplinary theoretical exploration and methodological innovation. Cognitive psy- chology provides a crucial theoretical foundation for compr

--- p.2 chars=5720 ---
Knowledge-Based Systems 315 (2025) 113281 2 Q. Wu et al. Fig. 1. Learners’ cognitive activities and knowledge states at each time step are influenced by extrinsic factors and intrinsic characteristics, respectively. Consequently, neglecting that situation precludes an accurate grasp of the essence of the learning process. In recent years, the field of cognitive psychology has witnessed a proliferation of studies exploring cognitive engagement and cognitive states, offering new perspectives for understanding cognitive dynamics in learning processes. Liu et al. [6] employed deep learning methods to identify learners’ cognitive states, revealing e-learning patterns under different cognitive engagement modes. Some studies focus on analyzing the associations between cognitive factors and learning outcomes, such as predicting academic performance based on emotions and cogni- tive engagement [7], and the impact of cognitive interference and emotional patterns on academic achievement [8]. Additionally, some scholars have proposed novel approaches like semi-supervised learn- ing for identifying cognitive patterns in online learning discussions, achieving favorable results [9]. These studies expand the application of cognitive modeling in intelligent education; however, they still lack a systematic consideration of the multi-dimensional influencing factors in complex learning environments. To overcome the limitations of existing KT methods, this paper introduces the concept of cognitive situation. A cognitive situation specif- ically refers to a learner’s cognitive processing in a particular spatiotem- poral situation, emphasizing the dynamic associations among extrinsic factors, intrinsic characteristics, cognitive activities, and knowledge states, thereby establishing a contextualized learner cognitive model. This concept provides a theoretical framework for analyzing key ele- ments and their interaction patterns in learning environments. Within a cognitive situation, it is posited that a learner’s knowledge state not only updates but also be influenced by intrinsic characteristics such as individual differences, perceptions of task difficulty, knowledge recep- tion capacity, and adaptation to teaching methods, leading to variations in knowledge states. Fig. 1 illustrates that at each time step, both knowledge states and cognitive activities change to varying degrees due to the influence of extrinsic factors and intrinsic characteristics. By fo- cusing on cognitive situations, a more comprehensive understanding of learners’ cognitive change patterns in complex learning environments can be achieved. This approach supports the design of personalized and effective learning strategies, thereby enhancing learners’ learning outcomes and mastery levels. Our specific contributions are described as follows: • By leveraging insights from cognitive psychology and typical application scenarios, we propose defining the cognitive situation and developing a correlation analysis model based on ‘‘extrin- sic factors-cognitive process-intrinsic characteristics’’. This model aims to enhance the alignment of KT tasks with real-world situa- tions. • CSGKT captures the schema of intrinsic features and extrinsic factors through HGNN modeling, and DGCNN is modeled to capture the correlation information between knowledge concepts and Erase-add Gate to rationalize learners’ cognition. • Extensive experiments were conducted on four benchmark datasets and compared with nine baseline models from three mainstream approaches. The experimental results show that CSGKT achieves the most advanced prediction performance. • Two major experiments in cognitive psychology were simulated to validate the two classic assertions: ‘‘short-term memory for- getting of knowledge concepts is mainly caused by interference rather than memory trace fading’’ and ‘‘cognitive imagery and perceptual functions play an equivalent role in the cognitive process’’. Section 2 presents the background information, while Section 3 elaborates on the KT problem and introduces the proposed CSGKT model. Section 4 explains the experimental setup, and Section 5 presents the experimental re- sults and analysis. Based on the KT results, Section 6 discusses the results of two simulation verification experiments for our approach. Section 7 is the conclusion. The paper ends with the discussion in Secti

Knowledge-Based Systems 315 (2025) 113281 3 Q. Wu et al. 2.1.1. Sequence-based approaches Neural networks are commonly used in the field of KT due to their superior ability to learn from sequential data, allowing them to effectively process large amounts of information. In comparison to traditional models, neural networks have significantly improved performance in this area. The Deep KT (DKT) model [12] utilizes a Recurrent Neural Network (RNN) to extract learners’ potential knowl- edge proficiency from their motion sequences. However, DKT has limitations such as relying on a single input, which can be addressed by incorporating various rich features [13,14] of learners to individ- ualize and account for differences between them [15]. The Learning and Forgetting behavior modeling for KT (LFKT) [16] model not only considers the forgetting factor but also combines learners’ mastery of each knowledge concept and their responses in real time to trace their learning progress. Another major limitation of DKT is that it cannot judge the mastery of a particular knowledge concept. The Dynamic Key- Value Memory Networks for KT (DKVMN) [17] model uses dynamic and static matrices to store and update learners’ mastery level of each knowledge concept, addressing this limitation. DKVMN is a memory network [18] that refers to the use of a dynamic matrix to address changing conditions in real time, while the static matrix stores more permanent information. The recent Question Difficulty Consistent KT (QDCKT) model [19] innovatively combines Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) [20,21] networks and feed-forward neural networks, replacing question IDs with question difficulty levels. This approach facilitates the transformation of model predictions into estimations of learners’ skill knowledge states. Sequence-based models rely on a single in- put, failing to capture the multidimensional nature of learning, which leads to an oversimplified representation of learners’ knowledge states, thereby affecting the effectiveness of learning and prediction accuracy. A study by Yeung et al. [22] demonstrated that such simplifications can result in up to a 15% reduction in prediction accuracy when dealing with complex, interdisciplinary subjects. Moreover, these models do not account for individual learning styles and cognitive processes, which can undermine the effectiveness of personalized learning strategies. Research by Zhang et al. [23] showed that adaptive learning systems based solely on sequence data failed to improve learning outcomes for up to 30% of students, particularly those with diverse learning needs. Therefore, it is crucial to develop more comprehensive and adaptable models to better capture the complex dynamics of the learning process. 2.1.2. Transformer -based approaches The Transformer [24] has been utilized in learners’ studying tasks. It incorporates a self-attention mechanism to enhance its ability to capture intrinsic relationships and interactions [25–27]. One of the most representative models is the Attentive KT (AKT) model [25], which has achieved advanced performance in KT tasks. Additionally, Pu et al. proposed a KT model that combines DKT and Transformer models, called DKT+Transformer [28]. This model utilizes the corre- lation between time intervals and knowledge concepts as input. The SAINT and SAINT+ models [29,30] suggest that current KT models based on attention mechanisms do not fully utilize deep self-attention mechanisms to capture the complex relationship between questions and answers. To address this, a new input method is adopted, and the use of attention layers is increased to improve the experimental results. HiTSKT [31] model employs a hierarchical Transformer architecture to capture both intra-session and inter-session knowledge dynamics. Additionally, it incorporates a power-law decay attention mechanism to simulate learners’ forgetting behavior. 2.1.3. Complex structure-based approaches Based on the Graph-based KT (GKT) [32], the GKT-CD [33] intro- duces cognitive diagnostic techniques to overcome the limitations of the traditional KT approach in analyzing learners’ abilities. It initially considered that learners would be affected by intrinsic characteris- tics, but did not explore them further. In Session Graph-based KT (SGKT) [34], on the other hand, the concept of a session graph is used to model the learner interaction process. This interaction mech- anism enables the knowledge states and embedded repr

--- p.4 chars=7031 ---
Knowledge-Based Systems 315 (2025) 113281 4 Q. Wu et al. Fig. 2. An ‘‘extrinsic factors-cognitive process-intrinsic characteristics’’ correlation analysis model. late in the evening, it might infer fatigue rather than lack of knowledge, and suggest a break or switch to a different topic. This nuanced approach could lead to more effective learning interventions and more accurate predictions of long-term learning outcomes. The concept of cognitive situation emphasizes that learning is a complex process involving the interplay of multiple factors, rather than a mere accumulation of knowledge. This aligns with contempo- rary learning science perspectives, such as the ‘‘How People Learn’’ framework proposed by Bransford et al. [50] and the situated learning theory of Lave and Wenger [51]. These theories collectively construct a multi-layered model of learning, underscoring the critical roles of environment, prior knowledge, motivation, and sociocultural situation in the learning process. They provide a robust theoretical underpinning for the conceptualization of cognitive situation. The complex composition of the cognitive situation covers several important dimensions. In Fig. 2, we visualize the correlation analysis model ‘‘extrinsic factors-cognitive process-intrinsic characteristics’’ for cognitive situations. This model specifies the interrelationships between the elements of the cognitive situation, and the green arrows indicate the unidirectional influence of the elements. Extrinsic factors, i.e., the organization of the physical environment and social interactions, can influence information acquisition and cognitive processes, including questions, teachers, assignments, opportunities, resources, etc., and have a clear impact on learners’ cognitive activities. The significance of these extrinsic influences is thoroughly explained in Bandura’s so- cial cognitive theory [52], which emphasizes the dynamic interplay among individual cognition, behavior, and environmental factors. In- trinsic characteristics, which cover the learner’s characteristics, include answers, abilities, thinking, resilience, and patience. These intrinsic characteristics not only reflect learners’ personalities but also influence their performance in cognitive tasks. Dweck’s Mindset Theory [53] and Duckworth’s research on grit [54] further explain the mecha- nisms through which these intrinsic characteristics influence learning outcomes. Cognitive processes are fundamental mechanisms through which humans perceive the extrinsic world and process information [4]. These processes reflect the dynamic changes in cognitive states, which comprise cognitive activities (such as attention, perception, memory, and reasoning) and knowledge states (including mastered concepts, skills, and experiences). These cognitive activities and knowledge states continuously interact and update during the learning process, forming complex cognitive processes. The model design in the cognitive situation is informed by two key cognitive psychology assertions. Firstly, the assertion that ‘‘short-term memory forgetting of knowledge concepts is mainly caused by interfer- ence rather than memory trace fading’’ emphasizes the importance of inter-concept interactions during the learning process. In the CSGKT model, the DGCNN component is designed to capture complex rela- tionships between different knowledge concepts, simulating potential interference and facilitation effects among concepts in actual learning scenarios. This design reflects our understanding of short-term memory forgetting mechanisms and underscores the necessity of considering dynamic interactions between concepts in KT. Secondly, the assertion that ‘‘cognitive imagery and perceptual functions play an equivalent role in the cognitive process’’ posits that intrinsic cognitive representations and extrinsic perceptual inputs play equally important roles in the learning process. Our model addresses this through the Heterogeneous Graph Neural Network (HGNN) com- ponent, which simultaneously processes learners’ intrinsic character- istics (analogous to cognitive representations) and extrinsic factors (analogous to perceptual functions), integrating them into a unified framework. This design reflects our consideration of the balanced role of intrinsic representations and extrinsic stimuli in cognitive processes. By incorporating these cognitive psychology theories into the model design, we attempt to create a KT system

Knowledge-Based Systems 315 (2025) 113281 5 Q. Wu et al. Fig. 3. An overview of the overall architecture of the CSGKT model. and intrinsic characteristics (Feature A, . . . , Feature N ) are quantized to obtain the extrinsic factor pattern 𝑧 and intrinsic feature pattern 𝑓 , respectively. 𝑧 and 𝑓 denote the combined pattern of extrinsic factor and intrinsic characteristic influences on the learner, respectively. The second module is to form the associated information. The input is the set of knowledge concepts 𝐶, which consists of a blend of question attributes and skill attributes from extrinsic factors, and answer at- tributes from intrinsic characteristics. This module uses DGCNN to learn the association between knowledge concepts and obtains the cognitive activities 𝑥𝑧, which reflects the learner’s state in the learning process, and the knowledge states 𝑥𝑓 , which reflects the learner’s mastery of knowledge. The third module is state evolution. The inputs are the four parameters obtained from the first and second modules. This module uses LSTM to train the temporal hidden states of the four parameters and obtains 𝑧ℎ𝑡, 𝑓ℎ𝑡, ℎ𝑧 𝑡 , and ℎ𝑓 𝑡 . The fourth module is stable cognition. The input is the hidden state obtained from the third module. This module uses the Erase-add Gate mechanism to filter out cognitive bias and match the cognitive activity with the knowledge state to get the final prediction result 𝑦𝑡. Next, we describe these four modules in detail. The pseudo-code of CSGKT is described as Algorithm 1. 3.1. Problem formulation In an online education platform, each question 𝑞𝑖 corresponds to one or more knowledge concepts. Learners acquire the relevant knowledge concepts by answering the questions. Let 𝑄 = {𝑞1,𝑞 2, …,𝑞𝑛} denote the question set containing 𝑛 questions, where 𝑞𝑖 denotes the 𝑖th question, which is a 𝑘-dimensional one-hot vector, i.e., 𝑞𝑖 ∈ R𝑘. The learner’s (question, answer) sequence is denoted as 𝑆 = {( 𝑞1,𝑎 1), (𝑞2,𝑎 2), …, (𝑞𝑡,𝑎𝑡)}, where 𝑞𝑖 ∈ 𝑄 denotes the question answered by the learner at timestamp 𝑖, and 𝑡 is the most recent timestamp. Each learner can answer the question of interest at a specific time and give a correct or incorrect response, denoted as 𝑎𝑖 ∈ {0, 1}. If 𝑎𝑖 = 0, the answer is wrong; if 𝑎𝑖 = 1, the answer is correct. Given a learner’s past learning sequence 𝑆 and a new question 𝑞𝑡+1, we need to model the learner’s historical learning sequences to trace the changes in their knowledge state over time. Our goal is to predict the probability 𝑃 (𝑎𝑡+1 = 1 ∣ 𝑆,𝑞𝑡+1) that the learner will answer the new question correctly based on the current knowledge state. 3.2. Forming schemas HGNN [55] extends the concept of traditional GNN to handle hy- pergraph data. In HGNN, both nodes and hyperedges of the hypergraph can carry multiple elements or attributes, and the connectivity relation- ships of the hyperedges can be more complex. The key idea of HGNN Algorithm 1 Cognitive Situation-based Graph Knowledge Tracing (CSGKT) Input: Extrinsic factor nodes set 𝑉𝑧, Intrinsic characteristic nodes set 𝑉𝑓 , Knowledge concept set 𝐶, Interaction sequence 𝑆 Output: Predicted result 𝑦𝑡 1: initialize node embeddings for 𝑉𝑧 and 𝑉𝑓 2: initialize hyperedge embeddings for hyperedges connecting nodes in 𝑉𝑧 and 𝑉𝑓 3: for node in 𝑉𝑧 do 4: 𝑉𝑧_𝑒𝑚𝑏𝑒𝑑𝑑𝑖𝑛𝑔 ←𝐸𝑚𝑏𝑒𝑑𝑑𝑖𝑛𝑔 (𝑛𝑜𝑑𝑒,ℎ𝑦𝑝𝑒𝑟𝑒𝑑𝑔𝑒𝑠 _𝑐𝑜𝑛𝑛𝑒𝑐𝑡𝑒𝑑 _𝑡𝑜_𝑛𝑜𝑑𝑒) 5: end for 6: for node in 𝑉𝑓 do 7: 𝑉𝑓 _𝑒𝑚𝑏𝑒𝑑𝑑𝑖𝑛𝑔 ←𝐸𝑚𝑏𝑒𝑑𝑑𝑖𝑛𝑔 (𝑛𝑜𝑑𝑒,ℎ𝑦𝑝𝑒𝑟𝑒𝑑𝑔𝑒𝑠 _𝑐𝑜𝑛𝑛𝑒𝑐𝑡𝑒𝑑 _𝑡𝑜_𝑛𝑜𝑑𝑒) 8: end for 9: 𝑧 ←ℎ𝑦𝑝𝑒𝑟𝑔𝑟𝑎𝑝ℎ(𝑉𝑧_𝑒𝑚𝑏𝑒𝑑𝑑𝑖𝑛𝑔 ) ⊳ Equation (3) 10: 𝑓 ←ℎ𝑦𝑝𝑒𝑟𝑔𝑟𝑎𝑝ℎ(𝑉𝑓 _𝑒𝑚𝑏𝑒𝑑𝑑𝑖𝑛𝑔 ) ⊳ Equation (4) 11: initialize directed edges based on the knowledge concept set 𝐶 12: for node in 𝐶 do 13: 𝐶_𝑒𝑚𝑏𝑒𝑑𝑑𝑖𝑛𝑔 ←𝐸𝑚𝑏𝑒𝑑𝑑𝑖𝑛𝑔 (𝑛𝑜𝑑𝑒,𝑑𝑖𝑟𝑒𝑐𝑡𝑒𝑑 _𝑒𝑑𝑔𝑒𝑠 ) 14: end for 15: 𝑥𝑧 ←𝑑𝑖𝑟𝑒𝑐𝑡𝑒𝑑 _𝑔𝑟𝑎𝑝ℎ_𝑐𝑜𝑛𝑣𝑜𝑙𝑢𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛(𝐶_𝑒𝑚𝑏𝑒𝑑𝑑𝑖𝑛𝑔,𝑧 ) ⊳ Equation (8) 16: 𝑥𝑓 ←𝑑𝑖𝑟𝑒𝑐𝑡𝑒𝑑 _𝑔𝑟𝑎𝑝ℎ_𝑐𝑜𝑛𝑣𝑜𝑙𝑢𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛(𝐶_𝑒𝑚𝑏𝑒𝑑𝑑𝑖𝑛𝑔,𝑓 ) ⊳ Equation (8) 17: for 𝑡 in range(len(𝑆)) do 18: 𝑧ℎ[𝑡],𝑓ℎ [𝑡],ℎ𝑧[𝑡],ℎ𝑓 [𝑡] ← 𝐿𝑆𝑇𝑀 (𝑧),𝐿𝑆𝑇𝑀 (𝑓 ),𝐿𝑆𝑇𝑀 (𝑥𝑧), 𝐿𝑆𝑇𝑀 (𝑥𝑓 ) ⊳ Equation (9) 19: end for 20: 𝜆𝑧,𝜆𝑥,𝜇𝑥,𝜇𝑓 ←𝐸𝑟𝑎𝑠𝑒𝐴𝑑𝑑 _𝐺𝑎𝑡𝑒(𝑧ℎ𝑡,ℎ𝑧 𝑡,ℎ𝑓 𝑡 ,𝑓ℎ 𝑡) ⊳ Equation (10) 21: 𝑦𝑡 ←𝑓𝑢𝑠𝑖𝑜𝑛 (𝜆𝑥,𝜆𝑧,𝜇𝑥,𝜇𝑓 ) ⊳ Equation (10) 22: return 𝑦𝑡 is to capture the relationships between the nodes in the hypergraph by designing appropriate hypergraph convolution operations. These convolution operations consider all the elements in the hyperedges to compute the representation of the nodes. Traditional graph struc- tures struggle to express the multifaceted relationships in cognitive situations. For instance, factors such a

Through the ‘‘node-edge-node’’ (NEN) transformation, we can efficiently extract high-order schema on the hypergraph. Specifically, suppose there are 𝑚 extrinsic factor nodes 𝑉𝑧 = {𝑣𝑧 1,𝑣𝑧 2, …,𝑣𝑧 𝑚} and 𝑛 intrinsic characteristic nodes 𝑉𝑓 = {𝑣𝑓 1,𝑣𝑓 2, …,𝑣𝑓 𝑛 }, where 𝑣𝑧 𝑖 denotes the 𝑖th 𝑚-dimensional extrinsic factor node and 𝑣𝑓 𝑗 denotes the 𝑗th 𝑛-dimensional intrinsic characteristic node. Table 1 lists the specific information of extrinsic factor nodes and intrinsic characteristic nodes. We integrate learning factors into the hypergraph structure through extrinsic factor nodes 𝑉𝑧 and intrinsic characteristic nodes 𝑉𝑓 . To construct the hypergraph structure, a similarity function 𝑆(𝑣𝑖,𝑣𝑗 ) is first defined to measure the association strength between nodes: 𝑆(𝑣𝑖,𝑣𝑗 ) = cos(𝑣𝑖,𝑣𝑗 ) = 𝑣𝑖 ⋅𝑣𝑗 ‖𝑣𝑖‖ ‖𝑣𝑗 ‖ (1) where cos represents cosine similarity. Subsequently, a threshold 𝜏 is set, and a hyperedge is created between nodes 𝑣𝑖 and 𝑣𝑗 when 𝑆(𝑣𝑖,𝑣𝑗 ) > 𝜏. This approach allows for the capture of complex multi- dimensional relationships, extending beyond simple binary relations. Extrinsic factor nodes 𝑉𝑧 correspond to extrinsic factors influencing the learning environment, encompassing ten aspects including ques- tions, assignments, teachers, classes, and networks. These factors influ- ence cognitive processes by stimulating learners’ cognitive activities. For instance, different tutoring modes require varying levels of cog- nitive resources, while question difficulty and relevant skills affect learners’ information processing demands. Factors such as networks and homework percentage also impact learners’ learning outcomes. Intrinsic characteristic nodes 𝑉𝑓 represent learners’ traits and cog- nition, comprising seven aspects including answer correctness, abil- ity, thinking time, and self-discipline. These intrinsic characteristics influence cognitive processes by shaping learners’ knowledge states and, more importantly, reflect individual differences among learners. For example, ability (user_id) can be viewed as a representation of learners’ prior knowledge and overall learning capacity; thinking time (first_response_time) and initial action (first_action) reflect learners’ cognitive processing speed and question-solving strategies, revealing different learning styles; self-discipline (bottom_hint) and patience (at- tempt_count) be related to learning motivation, demonstrating learn- ers’ persistence and help-seeking behaviors when facing challenges. The extrinsic factor node’s essential information transforms. method ensures that the hypergraph captures higher-order similarity relationships between nodes. To acquire the essential information contained within node 𝑣𝑧 𝑖 , we utilize embedding techniques to obtain the extrinsic factor schema 𝑧𝑖. (3). 𝑧𝑖 (𝑙+1) =𝛿((𝐷𝑧 𝑣)−1∕2𝐻𝑧𝑊𝑧(𝐷𝑧 𝑒 )−1∕2(𝐻𝑧)𝑇 (𝐷𝑧 𝑣)−1∕2𝑧𝑖 𝑙𝛩𝑙) (3) where 𝑧(𝑙+1) 𝑖 ,𝑧𝑙 𝑖 ∈ R𝑚, denote the input embedding and output embed- ding of the extrinsic factor node 𝑣𝑧 𝑖 between the two layers, respectively. 𝛩𝑙 ∈ R𝑚×𝑚 is a learnable parameter matrix of layer 𝑙 and the propaga- tion matrix within the node layer. 𝐻𝑧 ∈ R𝑚×|𝐸𝑧|, the node-hyperedge association matrix, represents the connectivity of the nodes and hyper- edges. 𝑊𝑧 ∈ R|𝐸𝑧|×|𝐸𝑧| is the hyperedge weight matrix. 𝛿(⋅) represents a nonlinear activation function such as LeakyReLU [56]. 𝐷𝑧 𝑣 ∈ R𝑚×𝑚, is the degree matrix of the extrinsic factor node 𝑣𝑧 𝑖 , and the diagonal elements denote the degree of 𝑣𝑧 𝑖 . 𝐷𝑧 𝑒 ∈ R|𝐸𝑧|×|𝐸𝑧|, is the degree matrix of the extrinsic factor hyperedges, and the diagonal elements denote the degree of the hyperedges. They denote the number of connections of nodes and hyperedges, and the characteristic aggregation of nodes and hyperedges is smoothed by taking the inverse of the number of nodes and hyperedges. Where 𝐷𝑧 𝑣 = ∑ 𝑒𝑗𝑊𝑧(𝑒)𝐻𝑧 𝑖𝑗 (𝑣𝑖 ∈𝑉𝑧,𝑒𝑗 ∈𝐸𝑧) and 𝐷𝑧 𝑒 = ∑ 𝑣𝑖𝐻𝑧 𝑖𝑗 (𝑣𝑖 ∈𝑉𝑧,𝑒𝑗 ∈𝐸𝑧). In Fig. 4, the extrinsic factor node’s essential information trans- forms. This is achieved through the use of the HGNN layer, which facilitates NEN transformations, thereby allowing for a more refined treatment of extrinsic factors and intrinsic characteristics. Specifically, the initial node’s essential information 𝑧𝑖𝑙 is filtered using a learnable processing matrix 𝛩𝑙, and the node’s essential information is gathered based on the hyperedge, resulting in the creation of the hyperedge feature 𝐻𝑧. By multiplying the filtered information and the hyper- edge feature, the essential information of the output node is obtained by aggregating the essential information of the relevant hyperedge. Consequently, the HGNN layer can be effectively utilized to extract the higher-order schema from both the EFHG and ICHG through NEN transformations. This enhances the overall efficacy of the approach. Forming associated information DGCNN [57] exploits directional information in directed graphs for node representation learning and graph classification tasks. The core idea of DGCNN is to aggregate the neighbor information of the nodes through a convolutional operation, which considers the direction of the edges. This allows DGCNN to distinguish between incoming and outgoing edges and thus better capture the topology of the directed graph. It uses a convolutional layer to process the neighbor information of the nodes to generate a representation of the nodes. Moreover, relationships between knowl

Learners’ mastery of knowledge concepts is influenced not only by historical learning information 𝑆 but also by extrinsic factors and intrinsic characteristics. 𝐷 ∈ R𝑑×𝑑 is the degree matrix, 𝐷𝑖𝑖 =∑ 𝑗 (𝐴′𝑖𝑗 +𝐴′𝑗𝑖 ). 𝑧𝑙 𝑖, 𝑓𝑙 𝑖 ∈ R𝑑×𝑑 are the node features of the previous layer. 𝛷,𝜓 ∈ R𝑑×𝑑 are the model parameters to be learned in the network. 3.4. State evolution KT is fundamentally a sequence modeling problem, where learners’ cognitive states change dynamically over time. In educational sce- narios, this change typically exhibits both long-term trends (such as gradual improvement in abilities) and short-term fluctuations (such as variations in attention). Numerous studies [12,58,59] have demon- strated that LSTM networks are well-suited for sequence prediction problems with long-term dependencies. The gating mechanisms and memory cell design of LSTM make it particularly adept at capturing both long-term and short-term dependencies in sequential data, making it highly suitable for modeling the evolution of cognitive states. Thus, we adopt LSTM to model the dynamic changes in extrinsic factor schema 𝑧, intrinsic characteristic schema 𝑓 , cognitive activity 𝑥𝑧, and knowledge state 𝑥𝑓 over time. During each training, it is necessary to trace the hidden state of each parameter at the 𝑡th timestamp, which is denoted as 𝑧ℎ𝑡, 𝑓ℎ𝑡, ℎ𝑧 𝑡 , and ℎ𝑓 𝑡 . Eq. (9) illustrates the updating process, taking the cognitive activity as an example. 𝑖𝑡 =𝜎(𝑥𝑧 𝑡𝑊𝑥𝑧,𝑖 +ℎ𝑧 𝑡−1𝑊ℎ𝑧,𝑖 +𝑏𝑖) 𝑓𝑡 =𝜎(𝑥𝑧 𝑡𝑊𝑥𝑧,𝑓 +ℎ𝑧 𝑡−1𝑊ℎ𝑧,𝑓 +𝑏𝑓 ) 𝑜𝑡 =𝜎(𝑥𝑧 𝑡𝑊𝑥𝑧,𝑜 +ℎ𝑧 𝑡−1𝑊ℎ𝑧,𝑜 +𝑏𝑜) 𝑐𝑡 =𝑓𝑡⊙𝑐𝑡−1 +𝑖𝑡⊙ tanh(𝑥𝑧 𝑡𝑊𝑥𝑧,𝑐 +ℎ𝑧 𝑡−1𝑊ℎ𝑧,𝑐 +𝑏𝑐 ) ℎ𝑧 𝑡 =𝑜𝑡⊙ tanh(𝑐𝑡) (9) where, 𝑖𝑡, 𝑓𝑡, and 𝑜𝑡 represent the input gate, forget gate, and output gate, respectively. The variable 𝑐𝑡 is the cell state vector, while 𝑊∗ ∈ R𝑑×𝑑 and 𝑏∗ ∈ R𝑑 are trainable network parameters. The function 𝜎(⋅) represents a non-linear activation function, such as Sigmoid. 3.5. Stable cognition In cognitive processes, learners’ cognitive activities and knowledge states are not always in perfect alignment. For instance, learners some- times overestimate their abilities (high cognitive activity with a low knowledge state) or underestimate themselves due to frustrations (low cognitive activity with a high knowledge state). Simply merging these two states lead to prediction biases. As observed in Fig. 3, various pa- rameters operate independently, advancing according to specific rules within their respective domains, potentially resulting in mismatches between learners’ knowledge states and cognitive activities. Inspired by the literature [17], an Erase-add Gate is designed to address this mismatch issue. The core concept of the Erase-add Gate is to align learners’ current knowledge states and cognitive activities by dynamically adjusting the contributions of different situational factors. This mechanism effectively resolves the mismatch problem by allowing

Experimental setting In this section, we introduced four datasets and nine models that need to be compared and described the details of the experimental settings. 4.1. Datasets To ensure a comprehensive evaluation of our model, we selected four publicly available datasets spanning multiple years: ASSISTments 2009, KDDCup2010, Statics2011, and ASSISTments2017. These datasets represent distinct temporal nodes in the evolution of educational tech- nology and learning paradigms. They encompass extensive interaction records between learners and authentic computer-based tutoring sys- tems across different periods. This temporal diversity renders them ideal for research in large-scale, long-term dynamic KT environments. Moreover, these datasets cover various educational domains with vary- ing difficulty levels, providing a robust foundation for assessing the generalizability of our research findings. Table 2 presents detailed statistics of these datasets, highlighting their unique characteristics and temporal contexts. ASSISTments2009(Assist2009)1 was collected during the 2009– 2010 school year and contained a total of 525,535 interactions in its first release, which included duplicates that were prone to unreliable results. In the experiments of this paper, an updated version [17] is used, which contains datasets with two different interaction modes: the ‘‘skill-builder’’ and the ‘‘non-skill-builder’’. The ‘‘skill-builder’’ dataset is used in the experiments because it fixes the data modeling problem and removes duplicate records. The updated dataset contains 4151 learners, 110 question labels, and 325,637 interactions. This dataset primarily focuses on secondary school mathematics, encompassing topics such as algebra, geometry, and number sense. The difficulty levels range from foundational to intermediate. The distribution of correct and incorrect answers exhibits a right-skewed distribution, indicating that the majority of questions have a relatively high rate of correct answers. ASSISTments2017(Assist2017)2 comes from the 2017 data mining competition on the ASSISTment platform. It contains 4117 different questions answered by 1709 learners over 102 knowledge concepts, 1 https://sites.google.com/site/assistmentsdata/home/assistment-2009- 2010-data/skill-builder-data-2009-2010 2 https://sites.google.com/view/assistmentsdataminingdataset?authuser=0 generating 942,816 interactions. As part of the data mining competi- tion, this dataset contains the most descriptive information from all the task datasets. Similar to Assist2009, this dataset also covers secondary school mathematics but encompasses a broader range of difficulty levels, spanning from foundational to advanced topics. The distribution of correct and incorrect answers more closely approximates a nor- mal distribution, indicating a more uniform distribution of question difficulties. Statics20113 comes from an engineering statics course at Carnegie Mellon University in Fall 2011. The original dataset consisted of 361,092 interactions, 335 learners, and 1224 questions. In the KT literature, this dataset is usually preprocessed [17] to obtain 1223 different questions. A total of 333 learners answered questions on 80 knowledge concepts. After preprocessing, the number of learner interactions was reduced by almost half to 189,297. This preprocessing was because most of the interactions lacked information about whether the question was answered correctly or not. The preprocessing only considered the first valid attempt.
