--- p.10 chars=8148 ---
Knowledge-Based Systems 315 (2025) 113281 10 Q. Wu et al. specific model components. The selected models have demonstrated su- perior performance in previous empirical studies, ensuring that CSGKT is benchmarked against high-performance standards in the field. DKT [12] represents a significant milestone in KT as the first model to apply Recurrent Neural Networks (RNN) to this field. Its selection is crucial for establishing a baseline for neural network approaches in KT. DKT’s use of one-hot skill encoding and hidden vector representations provides a fundamental comparison point for more complex models, allowing us to assess the advancements made since its introduction. DKVMN [17] was chosen for its innovative use of dynamic and static matrices to model learner knowledge. This model addresses a key limitation in earlier approaches by explicitly tracking mastery levels of individual knowledge concepts. Its inclusion enables us to evaluate the effectiveness of memory-augmented neural networks in KT tasks. SAKT [60] marks the introduction of attention mechanisms in KT. Its selection is vital for understanding how attention-based models improve upon sequence models like DKT. SAKT’s ability to weight the importance of items in long sequences offers insights into handling long-term dependencies in learning trajectories. AKT [25] represents a sophisticated integration of attention mech- anisms with psychometric models. We included AKT to assess the ben- efits of incorporating domain-specific knowledge (through the Rasch model) into neural architectures. Its novel monotonic attention mecha- nism provides a unique perspective on modeling forgetting in learning processes. GKT [32] was selected as a representative of graph-based ap- proaches in KT. Its use of Graph Neural Networks (GNN) to model skill dependencies aligns closely with our CSGKT model’s graph-based components. Comparing CSGKT with GKT allows us to evaluate the advancements made in graph-based knowledge representation. HawkesKT [47] introduces point processes to model temporal dy- namics in KT. Its inclusion enables us to compare our approach with models that explicitly focus on capturing complex temporal patterns in learning. This comparison is crucial for assessing CSGKT’s ability to model time-dependent aspects of learning. CL4KT [46] represents the application of contrastive learning in KT. We selected this model to evaluate how CSGKT compares with approaches that leverage advanced feature learning techniques. This comparison helps in assessing the robustness of our model’s feature representations. QDCKT [19] was chosen for its explicit consideration of question difficulty in learner performance modeling. This model allows us to compare how CSGKT handles varying task complexities without explic- itly modeling question difficulty, providing insights into our model’s adaptability to diverse learning materials. HiTSKT [31] employs a hierarchical temporal self-attention mech- anism, representing an advanced approach to modeling long-term de- pendencies. Its inclusion allows us to assess CSGKT’s performance in capturing knowledge state changes over extended periods, a critical aspect of comprehensive KT. Through comparison with these models, we can comprehensively evaluate our proposed model’s capabilities in capturing the complexity of learning processes, handling temporal information, and utilizing knowledge structures. Simultaneously, this multi-dimensional compar- ison aids in accurately identifying our innovations and contributions, while validating the model’s effectiveness and novelty in KT tasks. 4.3. Setup and implementation Before the experiment, the training datasets and test datasets are split by extracting 80% of the sequences from the datasets as the former and reserving the remaining 20% as the latter. We embedded the problem concept representation into a 256- dimensional space to obtain the optimal hyperparameters. For HGNN and DGCNN, a two-layer GNN was employed for node embedding, while the LSTM model utilized a hidden layer and 128 hierarchical cells. The remaining models adopted the settings described in the original paper or used the AUC values obtained on the validation datasets. In the HGNN, the optimal similarity threshold 𝜏 was determined to be 0.6 through grid search. For the DGCNN, a 3-layer structure was adopted, effectively capturing multi-hop relationships without over- fitting. The normalization parameter 𝑝 for the relationship streng

While DKVMN uses discrete key–value memory to store static and dynamic information, CSGKT offers a more comprehensive approach by simultaneously modeling the interplay between evolving curricular content (extrinsic factors) and learners’ developing problem-solving competencies (intrinsic charac- teristics) in relation to progressively complex curricular content. This dynamic interaction modeling enables educators to not only identify the onset of learning difficulties but also to understand their underlying causes—whether they stem from knowledge gaps, necessitate alter- native instructional approaches, or result from temporal changes in learners’ cognitive strategies. For instance, on the Statics2011 dataset, learners’ ability (user_id) and thinking time (first_response_time) showed strong correlations with their per- formance, with correlation coefficients of 0.72 and −0.56 respectively. This indicates that learners with higher ability tend to have more stable and advanced knowledge states, while longer thinking times often correlate with less confident knowledge states. In contrast, on the Assist2017 dataset, the model’s AUC value was 0.7734 with an ACC of 0.6943, relatively lower. This reflects the complexity of primary and secondary education environments. At this stage, learners’ studies are influenced by more situational factors, increasing the difficulty of prediction. Simultaneously, the lower RMSE value indicates that despite challenges, the model’s prediction errors remain relatively small. This performance corresponds to the actual situation in primary and secondary education, where learners’ learning trajectories tend to be more variable. The model’s performance was equally outstanding on the KDD- Cup2010 dataset, with an AUC of 0.8158 and ACC of 0.7268. This dataset had the lowest RMSE value (0.3438), indicating more precise predictions by the model. This reflects a more structured educational environment represented by this dataset, with more efficient learning behaviors among the learner population. These results not only demonstrate the model’s technical perfor- mance but, more importantly, reflect its adaptability to different ed- ucational stages and learning environments. Cognitive situation modeling combines extrinsic factors, such as curricular content and resource availability, with intrinsic characteristics, including prior knowledge and learning styles. This integration provides a more comprehensive framework for addressing heterogeneous and dynamic learning envi- ronments. For instance, in the Assist2017 dataset, which represents primary and secondary education, CSGKT demonstrates adaptability to the rapidly evolving learning environments of younger learners. It takes into account factors such as fluctuations in assignment difficulty and the development of learners’ self-management skills. Consequently, the model more accurately predicts performance variations across di- verse subjects and learning stages. This capability distinguishes CS- GKT from models like CL4KT, which primarily focus on characteristic representation without considering the full spectrum of situational factors. In F1 score, CSGKT performs best on Statics2011 and KDDCup2010 datasets, demonstrating a good balance between precision and recall. However, on Assist2009 and Assist2017 datasets, CSGKT’s F1 scores are slightly lower than the CL4KT model. This is due to these datasets containing more diverse problem types and learner groups, increasing the difficulty of accurate predictions. CSGKT consistently maintains superior F1 scores across diverse datasets, as shown by its performance on Assist2009 (0.7879 compared to GKT’s 0.7543). This demonstrates CSGKT’s balanced approach in identifying both mastered and non- mastered concepts. Such balance surpasses graph-based models like GKT, which occasionally overemphasize structural relationships at the expense of crucial characteristics. CSGKT’s superior performance in capturing the influence of intrinsic characteristics on knowledge states is particularly evident. For exam- ple, unlike models such as DKT or DKVMN which primarily focus on sequential patterns, CSGKT successfully modeled the impact of learn- ers’ self-discipline (bottom_hint) on their knowledge state evolution, revealing a moderate positive correlation (0.43) with performance improvement over time across all datasets. CSGKT achieves multi-dimensional, dynamic modeling of the learn- ing process by integrating HGNN, DGCNN, LSTM, and Erase-Add Gating units. This complex structure gives CSGKT an advantage in capturing cognitive states and learning process dynamics, especially when dealing with complex learning environments and knowledge structures. For example, HGNN allows the model to simultaneously consider learners’ intrinsic characteristics and extrinsic factors, which is particularly im- portant in professional course datasets like Statics2011, where learners’ prior knowledge and learning environment significantly impact learn- ing outcomes. The application of LSTM enhances the model’s adaptabil- ity to dynamic learning processes, which is particularly advantageous in long-term learning datasets like KDDCup2010. Howeve

AUC was selected as the primary evaluation metric due to its comprehensive reflection of model performance across different thresholds, unaffected by class imbalance. To ensure result reliability, 20 independent experiments were conducted, each initializing model parameters with different random seeds. Table 4 presents the paired 𝑡-test results of CSGKT against various baseline models on the AUC metric, including 𝑡-statistics, 𝑝-values, Co- hen’s 𝑑 effect sizes, and 95% confidence intervals for AUC differences. The interpretation standards for effect size 𝑑 are: 0.2 indicates a small effect, 0.5 a medium effect, and 0.8 a large effect. Results demonstrate that CSGKT significantly outperforms baseline models in the majority of cases (𝑝< 0.05), with 95% confidence intervals not including 0, further confirming the reliability of performance differences. The results demonstrate that CSGKT significantly outperforms base- line models in the vast majority of cases (𝑝 < 0.05), with 95% confi- dence intervals excluding 0, further confirming the model’s reliability. On the Assist2009 dataset, CSGKT exhibits extremely high statistical significance and large effect sizes compared to all baseline models. For instance, against DKT, CSGKT’s AUC improvement has a 95% confidence interval of [0.102, 0.134], indicating that in practical ap- plications, CSGKT correctly identifies 10.2% to 13.4% more learners’ knowledge states than DKT. This improvement in large-scale educa- tional systems translates to thousands of learners receiving more precise learning guidance. The Statics2011 dataset, representing engineering courses in higher education, showcases CSGKT’s most significant advantages. Compared to GKT, the confidence interval for AUC improvement is [0.107, 0.134]. This substantial improvement indicates that CSGKT more accurately traces learners’ knowledge mastery in complex engineering concept learning, crucial for adjusting the teaching pace, identifying learners needing additional support, and personalizing assignment allocation. This reflects the complexity and diversity of primary and secondary education environments. This underscores the need to select or combine different models based on specific educational stages and subject characteristics when designing adaptive learning systems. Results on the KDDCup2010 dataset are particularly noteworthy. CSGKT shows significant advantages over most models, but the dif- ference with AKT is not significant (CI: [-0.003, 0.004]). Given this dataset’s characteristics (containing numerous algebra questions), this indicates that in highly structured subjects with clear logical relation- ships, different advanced models reach similar performance ceilings. Notably, CSGKT’s ability to model the impact of intrinsic character- istics on knowledge states contributes to its statistical superiority. For instance, on the Statics2011 dataset, CSGKT’s representation of the relationship between thinking time and knowledge state accuracy was significantly more precise than that of CL4KT (𝑝 < 0.001, 𝑑 = 3.07), highlighting CSGKT’s enhanced capability in modeling cognitive processes. Model analysis To further investigate the effectiveness of the four key components (schema formation using HGNN, association information formation us- ing DGCNN, temporal modeling using LSTM, and cognitive stabilization using Erase-add Gate) of the CSGKT model, four variants of the CSGKT model were established. Their performance across different datasets is reported in Table 6. • CSGKT-RH (Remove the forming schema component) For the CSGKT-RH variant, we do not utilize HGNN to extract schema for the extrinsic factors and intrinsic characteristics. The purpose of this variant is to evaluate the impact of using complex schema extracted by HGNN on the performance of CSGKT. • CSGKT-RD (Remove the forming associated information compo- nent) For CSGKT-RD, we do not use DGCNN to extract the related information of concepts contained in the questions. The purpose of this variant is to evaluate the impact of using DGCNN to extract the dependency relationships between the concepts contained in the questions on the performance of CSGKT. • CSGKT-RL (Remove the temporal modeling component): For CSGKT-RL, LSTM is replaced with a simple feed-forward neural network for temporal modeling. This variant aims to evaluate the role of LSTM in capturing long-term dependencies in the learning process. • CSGKT-RE (Remove the stabilizing cognition component) For CSGKT-RE, we do not use the Erase-add Gate to filter out knowl- edge states that do not match the learner’s current cognitive activity. The purpose of this variant is to evaluate the impact of using the Erase-add Gate to mitigate the bias introduced by self-training on the performance of CSGKT. The various components of the CSGKT model play unique and important roles in the KT task. The experimental results in Table 6 demonstrate the contribution of each component to the model’s performance. The performance decline of CSGKT-RH is relatively small, especially on the Assist2017 and Statics2011 datasets, indicating that HGNN con- tributes to capturing learning environments and individual cognition. Although HGNN’s impact is less pronounced than other components, its ability to process situational factors, coupled with its moderate computational complexity 𝑂(𝑚⋅|𝐸𝑧|⋅𝑑+𝑛⋅|𝐸𝑓 |⋅𝑑), remains an important part of the model. CSGKT-RD shows the most significant performance drop, partic- ularly on the Assist2017 dataset. This suggests that DGCNN plays a crucial role in modeling knowledge concept relationships, with its 𝑂(𝑘2⋅ 𝑑2) complexity yielding substantial performance returns in practice. CSGKT-RL exhibits a slight performance decrease, indicating that LSTM’s temporal modeling capability contr

Effect of stabilizing cognition We sequentially selected thirty questions and their answers from the Assist2009 dataset. Fig. 8 shows the prediction results of the CSGKT-RE model and the CSGKT model after 50 iterations. This heatmap presents the prediction patterns of the models with and without the Erase-add Gate on a continuous sequence of questions. It can be visually observed that as questions change, such as from (6, 1) to (9, 0), although the color gradients of corresponding blocks differ between the two models, the degree of change in color gradi- ents between adjacent blocks is similar, indicating consistency in the fluctuation of prediction results. However, the CSGKT model exhibits noticeably more stable fluctuations, as seen in the range (7, 1) to (42, 0). The CSGKT-RE model shows more abrupt jumps in changes, particularly in the ranges (82, 0) to (6, 1) and (30, 1) to (42, 0). This comparison visually demonstrates the effectiveness of the Erase-add Gate in maintaining prediction stability. The stabilizing effect of the Erase-add Gate contributes to improved prediction accuracy in several key aspects: Firstly, it smooths out short-term anomalous fluctuations, preventing the model from overreacting to temporary changes in cognitive states. Secondly, it enables the model to better capture the gradual changes in learners’ cognitive states, which aligns more closely with the characteristics of knowledge accumulation in actual learning processes. Finally, this stability mechanism likely helps the model maintain robustness when faced with noisy data, such as when learners occasionally provide results inconsistent with their current knowledge state. This not only enhances the overall prediction accuracy of the model but also provides educators with a more stable assessment of learners’ cognitive states, facilitating the development of more coherent and effective teaching strategies. 6. Cognitive psychology validation experiment The KT task can reflect subtle changes in learners’ cognitive pro- cesses, and the results help explore the degree of deviation that differ- ent individuals produce in the mean process. We conduct the following two cognitive psychology validation experiments. 6.1. Short-term memory forgetting of knowledge concepts is mainly caused by interference rather than memory trace fading During the cognitive process, learners engage in information pro- cessing, comparison, and judgment, and must concentrate on this task. This process involves various sources of interference information [61], including task interference (other tasks or information interfering with the current task) and intrinsic interference (such as emo

This difference reflects the model’s ability to distinguish between interference effects and natural memory decay, consistent with the observed experimental phenomena. The experimental results not only support the theory that short-term memory forgetting is primarily caused by interference but also validate the effectiveness of our model in simulating different types of cogni- tive processes. This finding emphasizes the importance of considering inter-concept interference in KT tasks, providing direction for further optimization of model structures to more accurately predict learning states. 6.2. Cognitive imagery and perceptual function play an equivalent role in the cognitive process Cognitive imagery [63,64] refers to the mental intrinsic representa- tion generated by learners through intrinsic thinking and imagination, which is flexible and variable in terms of manipulation and change. On the other hand, the perceptual function [65,66] denotes the learners’ ability to perceive the extrinsic world and convert extrinsic percep- tual information into neural signals. As perceptual function passively receives and processes extrinsic information, it remains stable and invariant. The study simulates the effects of cognitive imagery and perceptual function on the cognitive process by controlling intrinsic characteristics and extrinsic factors. In our model, the HGNN component is designed to simultane- ously process intrinsic characteristics and extrinsic factors. Intrinsic characteristics can be analogized to cognitive imagery, reflecting learn- ers’ intrinsic cognitive levels, while extrinsic factors are analogous to perceptual functions, representing extrinsic stimuli in the learning environment. By integrating these two types of information within the same graph structure, HGNN provides a technical foundation for validating the equivalent roles of cognitive imagery and perceptual functions. To more precisely simulate the roles of cognitive imagery and perceptual function, we have implemented a fine-grained masking method. Specifically, we introduce masking matrices 𝑀𝑓 and 𝑀𝑧, which have dimensions identical to the intrinsic characteristics vector 𝑓𝑙 𝑖 and extrinsic factor vector 𝑧𝑙 𝑖, respectively. These masking matrices contain elements of 0 or 1, enabling selective masking of specific characteristics or factors through element-wise multiplication (⊙). For masking cognitive imagery (intrinsic characteristics), we employ the following formula: 𝑓𝑙+1 𝑖 =𝛿((𝐷𝑣 𝑓 )−1∕2𝐻𝑓𝑊𝑓 (𝐷𝑒 𝑓 )−1∕2(𝐻𝑓 )𝑇 (𝐷𝑣 𝑓 )−1∕2(𝑓𝑙 𝑖 ⊙𝑀𝑓 )𝛺𝑙) (18) Similarly, for masking perceptual function (extrinsic factors), we uti- lize: 𝑧𝑙+1 𝑖 =𝛿((𝐷𝑣 𝑧)−1∕2𝐻𝑧𝑊𝑧(𝐷𝑒 𝑧)−1∕2(𝐻𝑧)𝑇 (𝐷𝑣 𝑧)−1∕2(𝑧𝑙 𝑖⊙𝑀𝑧)𝛩𝑙) (19) This methodology allows for flexible control over the masking process. For instance, given an n-dimensional intrinsic characteristics vector 𝑓𝑙 𝑖 , we can set 𝑀𝑓 = [0, 1, 1, …, 1]𝑇 to mask the first characteristic while preserving others. Similarly, we can configure various patterns for 𝑀𝑧 to mask extrinsic factors. This fine-grained masking approach enables us to more accurately model scenarios where cognitive imagery and perceptual function are partially absent or diminished in real-world settings, thereby better reflecting the complexities of authentic learning

--- p.19 chars=4424 ---
Knowledge-Based Systems 315 (2025) 113281 19 Q. Wu et al. Fig. 10. The role of cognitive imagery and perceptual function in the cognitive process was validated through a kite plot. changes. CSGKT constructs a correlation analysis model of extrinsic factors, cognitive processes, and intrinsic characteristics with the help of cognitive psychology theories, and improves the ability to describe complex learning environments. CSGKT uses HGNN and DGCNN to model the influence and knowledge structure of learners, LSTM for state tracing, and Erase-add Gate for cognitive adjustment and realizes the simulation of dual state changes under the dual influence of learners. Through training and testing on four real datasets, the results show that the model’s KT performance is significantly better than that of the traditional deep learning model, the attention-based model, and the graph-based model, and realizes the prediction of state objects. The analysis of the effectiveness of the model on each component shows that the modeling of extrinsic factors and intrinsic characteristics, the learning of knowledge associations, and the cognitive regulation mech- anism all have a significant effect on the performance of the model. The simulation of two major experiments in cognitive psychology verifies that the main cause of short-term memory forgetting of knowledge concepts is interference rather than memory trace fading and that cognitive imagery and perceptual function are equivalent to cognitive processes, indicating that the model can reflect cognitive laws. In terms of time complexity, this method extracts features and association information through parallel HGNN and DGCNN and then trains them through LSTM, whose time complexity is 𝑂(𝑛), which is better than the traditional RNN method with serial training. In terms of spatial complexity, this method constructs an overall associated dual- state graph structure to express the learning process, which is more comprehensive than the single-state expression, but the consumption of computational resources is correspondingly larger. From a comprehen- sive point of view, the proposed method can control time consumption through parallel training while guaranteeing the expression ability, which has certain advantages of time and space efficiency. CSGKT demonstrates excellent performance across datasets from various disciplines such as mathematics and engineering, indicating its potential for broad application in diverse educational domains. The model’s core components (HGNN, DGCNN, LSTM, and Erase-add Gate) exhibit strong generalizability, capable of adapting to different types of learning data and knowledge structures. However, some adjustments are necessary when applying the model to new fields. These adjust- ments include modifying feature definitions based on cognitive situa- tions, optimizing knowledge structure representations, adjusting time scales, considering computational resource limitations, and adapting to varying data quality and quantity. Despite these potential adjustment needs, the fundamental framework and principles of CSGKT should apply to a wide range of educational domains. Moreover, CSGKT is not merely a prediction tool but can also pro- vide rich information for adaptive intervention strategies. By analyzing the outputs of various model components, personalized feedback and instructional adjustment strategies can be designed. For instance, the output from HGNN can reveal interactions between different learning factors, aiding in the construction of comprehensive teaching inter- vention strategies, including adjustments to teaching pace, matching of learning partners, and selection of teaching methods. The output from DGCNN can be used to analyze learners’ transfer abilities be- tween different knowledge concepts, providing guidance for designing knowledge integration and application exercises. The output from the Erase-Add Gate reflects the gap between learners’ current cognitive activities and ideal knowledge states, offering a scientific basis for formulating personalized learning methods. 8. Discussion 8.1. Theoretical contributions and practical implications This study significantly advances the field of KT across theoretical, methodological, and practical dimensions through the introduction of the cognitive situation concept and the CSGKT model.

--- p.20 chars=8354 ---
Knowledge-Based Systems 315 (2025) 113281 20 Q. Wu et al. Theoretically, the cognitive situation concept expands the KT theo- retical framework. This innovative concept integrates learners’ extrin- sic factors, intrinsic characteristics, and cognitive processes, offering a multidimensional perspective for KT tasks. The CSGKT model, by simultaneously considering environmental influences and individual cognitive characteristics, addresses the limitations of traditional KT methods that often neglect the complexity of learning environments. This approach facilitates a shift in KT research from static knowledge assessment to dynamic learning process analysis. Methodologically, the CSGKT model innovatively combines ad- vanced algorithms such as HGNN, DGCNN, LSTM, and Erase-add Gate, achieving effective modeling of complex learning environments. This integrated approach not only enhances predictive performance but also improves model interpretability. Notably, the simulation of two cog- nitive psychology assertions provides new directions for future KT re- search, potentially facilitating more accurate learning state assessments and the development of personalized instructional strategies. In terms of application, the CSGKT model offers crucial guidance for designing intelligent educational systems. Its multidimensional an- alytical capability enables dynamic adjustments of learning content difficulty and presentation modes, adapting to learners’ cognitive states and environmental factors. This real-time adaptive capacity signif- icantly enhances the practical value of KT in educational settings, promoting a transition towards truly personalized learning experiences in intelligent education. Moreover, this research strengthens interdisciplinary connections between KT and cognitive science by validating classic cognitive psy- chology hypotheses. This theoretical validation not only reinforces the scientific foundation of the CSGKT model but also indicates future research directions for integrating cognitive theories with machine learning techniques. This interdisciplinary approach has the potential to propel KT beyond mere performance prediction towards a deeper un- derstanding of learning mechanisms, providing more robust theoretical support for educational practices. 8.2. Limitations and future research directions Despite the significant advancements made in cognitive modeling and KT, this study has several limitations that require improvement and expansion in future research. Firstly, while this study focuses on extrinsic factors, intrinsic char- acteristics, and cognitive processes within cognitive situations, it lacks sufficient exploration of cognitive load issues in the learning process. According to cognitive load theory, learners have limited working memory capacity when processing information. When the complexity of learning tasks exceeds the learner’s processing ability, excessive cognitive load can negatively impact learning and memory. Future re- search endeavors could further refine cognitive load theory and develop dynamic cognitive load estimation algorithms within the CSGKT frame- work. These advancements could leverage physiological measurement data to provide real-time cognitive load assessments. Such innovations may potentially enhance intelligent educational systems, enabling auto- matic adjustments of task difficulty and presentation modalities based on learners’ current cognitive load. This adaptive approach would aim to ensure optimal challenge levels and maximize learning efficiency. Secondly, this study primarily concentrates on multi-perspective KT within a single domain, with insufficient attention to cross-domain or cross-task knowledge transfer issues. Learning transfer, the ability of learners to apply acquired knowledge and skills to new situations or questions, is key to effective learning. Future research could explore how to utilize the CSGKT model to support knowledge transfer, such as introducing transfer learning techniques to enhance model adaptability to new learners or domains through cross-domain knowledge mapping and sharing. Additionally, the role of metacognitive strategies in knowl- edge transfer could be investigated to help learners better monitor and regulate their learning processes, promoting deeper understanding and application. We propose evaluating the CSGKT model’s efficacy in predicting knowledge transfer across related yet distinct domains, such as the transition from

===== A Cross-Domain Knowledge Tracing Model Based on Graph Optimal Transport.pdf pages=25 =====

--- p.1 chars=2845 ---
World Wide Web (2025) 28:10 https://doi.org/10.1007/s11280-024-01311-1 A cross-domain knowledge tracing model based on graph optimal transport Zhengyang Wu 1,2 · Yuqi Liu 2 · Jianwei Cen 2 · Zetao Zheng 3 · Guandong Xu 4 Received: 31 July 2024 / Revised: 10 October 2024 / Accepted: 15 October 2024 / Published online: 26 December 2024 © The Author(s), under exclusive licence to Springer Science+Business Media, LLC, part of Springer Nature 2024 Abstract Knowledge tracing (KT) is an essential technology in intelligent education that predicts stu- dents’ performance by analyzing their learning behavior data. Despite signiﬁcant advances in KT, most existing research focuses on improving performance within a single domain (i.e., a speciﬁc academic discipline or school), with limited attention to Cross-Domain Knowl- edge Tracing (CDKT). CDKT aims to efﬁciently transfer the capabilities of a KT model from a source domain to a target domain. The main challenge in CDKT is aligning the knowledge state distributions between the two domains to compensate for the lack of inter- action data in the target domain. To address this challenge, we propose an Auto-encoder Embedding and Graph Optimal Transport based CDKT model (AEGOT-CDKT). In the ini- tial pre-training phase, we use an auto-encoder to learn the embedding representations of exercises and knowledge concepts in both the source and target domains. Next, we design a cross-domain alignment module that aligns the embedding representations of the two domains using the Graph Optimal Transport (GOT) strategy. This approach effectively supplements students’ knowledge state representations acquired from both domains and guides the learn- ing process in the target domain. Additionally, intra-domain alignment is conducted through a graph decoding objective, bringing node features with similar knowledge concepts or exer- cises closer together. Finally, we train an LSTM model using the aligned source domain embeddings and use the trained LSTM to predict student responses in the target domain. Transfer experiments in different educational contexts demonstrate that our approach suc- cessfully accomplishes CDKT task and validates the effectiveness of aligning knowledge state distributions across different domains. Keywords Knowledge tracing · Graph optimal transport · Cross-domain alignment · Graph attention network 1 Introduction With the rapid global digitalization and integration of intelligent technologies, smart online education is increasingly pivotal in shaping the education sector. Educational platforms have grown signiﬁcantly due to digital transformation, providing diverse online learning resources and convenient pathways. While these platforms have revolutionized traditional education Extended author information available on the last page of the article 123

--- p.2 chars=2580 ---
10 Page 2 of 25 World Wide Web (2025) 28 :10 and optimized resource utilization, they still face challenges in meeting personalized learning needs. To address diverse learning demands and vast knowledge, online education platforms must accurately trace and understand students’ mastery across various subjects. However, traditional knowledge tracing (KT) methods struggle with data discrepancies between dif- ferent disciplines and often perform poorly in new subject areas lacking sufﬁcient data. This highlights the necessity for cross-domain knowledge tracing (CDKT). Researchers are now exploring transfer learning to enhance teaching across domains by leveraging knowledge from speciﬁc areas. Transfer learning aims to transfer knowledge from one domain to another to enhance performance in the target domain [ 1]. Transfer learning can be applied in various domains including computer vision [ 2], speech recognition [ 3], equipment fault diagnosis [ 4–6], and recommendation systems [ 7]. In the ﬁeld of KT, Transfer learning techniques are widely applied to address CDKT challenges, aiming to effectively leverage data and knowledge from different domains to enhance model generalization and prediction accuracy. To the best of our knowledge, existing CDKT methods include kernel methods [8], adversarial learning- based methods [ 9, 10], graph neural network-based methods [ 11], and domain alignment- based methods [ 12]. We have decided to combine Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) with domain alignment to achieve CDKT. This is because the ﬁeld of knowledge tracing involves heterogeneous data such as students, knowledge concepts, and exercises, including complex relationships like students’ answer histories, associations between exercises and knowledge concepts, and dependencies among different knowledge concepts, which can naturally be represented using nodes and edges in a graph. We use GNNs to learn features of these nodes. Building upon this, we align feature vectors from two different domains using an explicit distribution distance metric, aiming to minimize domain discrepancies and achieve maximal cross-domain knowledge transfer. Figure 1 is an example of CDKT, knowledge concept pairs in the mathematical domain (e.g., vector, matrix) and the programming domain (i.e., 1D array, 2D array) are not only semantically similar but also have a progressive relationship in terms of their topological structures. Therefore, they can be matched for knowledge transfer. Figure 1 Illustration of aligning nodes and edges from two domains through graph matching 123

--- p.3 chars=3563 ---
World Wide Web (2025) 28 :10 Page 3 of 25 10 Based on the above considerations, this paper proposes a cross-domain based knowledge tracing, namely AEGOT-CDKT. This approach ﬁrst utilizes graph auto-encoders to con- struct knowledge tracing node-attribute graphs in the source and target domains. It captures and quantiﬁes the intricate dependencies and weight allocations among entities within each domain through a graph attention mechanism, thereby generating high-quality embedding vector representations. Subsequently, to facilitate effective knowledge transfer between these two domains, we have devised a cross-domain alignment module that treats the cross-domain alignment issue as a graph matching task. It leverages Graph Optimal Transport (GOT) [ 13] to establish the correspondence between nodes and edges of the knowledge tracing node- attribute relationship graphs in the source and target domains. Speciﬁcally, GOT employs Wasserstein Distance (WD) [14] to measure and match nodes in different domains, ensuring information consistency at the node level. Additionally, introducing Gromov-Wasserstein Distance (GWD) [ 15] addresses the edge matching challenge, guaranteeing precise align- ment of cross-domain data at the edge structure level within the graph. By combining WD and GWD, GOT can achieve maximum matching of corresponding nodes in terms of seman- tic information and topological structure in the graph, ultimately achieving a more reﬁned cross-domain alignment. In summary, the contributions of this study are threefold: • This paper proposes a novel CDKT model based on graph contrasting and transfer learn- ing, which predicts the performance of the next stage of students’ learning in the different domain. • The cross-domain alignment method employed in our model effectively reduces domain distribution discrepancies, facilitating knowledge transfer across different domains. • This paper reports on the extensive experiments that were conducted in three real datasets. The experiments verify that the proposed model is more effective than the existing KT methods in CDKT task. The rest of the paper is organized as follows. Section 2 brieﬂy reviews the related work. Section 3 introduce the problem statement. Section 4 describes the framework and the imple- mentation of the proposed approach in detail. Extensive experimental studies to validate the effectiveness of our model and several research questions are addressed in Section 5. Section 6 concludes this work, analyzes the study’s limitations and proposes future research directions. 2 Relatedwork 2.1 Traditional knowledge tracing method Knowledge tracing (KT) relies on real-time interactions between students and exercises to monitor and record their learning progress, thereby evaluating their mastery of knowledge. The academic community has accumulated extensive research ﬁndings on KT, which contin- ues to exhibit a consistent momentum of innovation and development. Corbett and Anderson [16] pioneered the Bayesian Knowledge Tracing (BKT) model in 1994, emphasizing the hier- archical structure of knowledge concepts and the structured learning experiences required for knowledge concept mastery [ 17, 18]. Deep learning technologies, particularly Deep Knowledge Tracing (DKT) introduced by Piech et al. [ 19] in 2015, have signiﬁcantly advanced the ﬁeld. DKT, based on recurrent neural networks, is renowned for its ability to model knowledge states dynamically, offering ﬂexibility and strong predictive performance. Subsequent advancements include Dynamic 123

--- p.4 chars=3812 ---
10 Page 4 of 25 World Wide Web (2025) 28 :10 Key-V alue Memory Networks (DKVMN) by Zhang et al. [20], which utilize memory matri- ces to store knowledge concepts (Key matrix) and update mastery levels (V alue matrix), and MA-LSTM by Li et al. [ 21], featuring a memory-aware module using temporal atten- tion layers to simulate human brain recall processes in KT. Chen et al. [ 22] proposed a knowledge tracing model with learning and forgetting behavior (LFBKT), which compre- hensively considers factors affecting learning and forgetting. By modeling students’ learning and forgetting behaviors, it traces changes in students’ knowledge states and predicts their performance. CAKT [ 23] enhances interpretability by integrating contrastive learning with attention networks. It employs three attention-based encoders to model the dynamic factors of Item Response Theory (IRT) [ 24] and effectively identify the consistency and separa- bility of knowledge states and learning interactions, leading to improved knowledge state representations. 2.2 Graph-based knowledge tracing method In educational data science, traditional deep learning methods often convert exercise records into linear knowledge sequences. However, many educational datasets are better represented as non-Euclidean structured graphs [25]. The Graph-based Knowledge Tracing model (GKT), proposed by Nakagawa et al. [26], adopts GNN principles by representing course knowledge concepts as graphs and framing KT problems as time-series node classiﬁcation tasks within these graphs. This approach has proven effective in KT tasks. Some KT studies utilize bipartite graphs and employ Graph Convolutional Network (GCN) embedding propagation in graph models with diverse nodes and edges to extract effec- tive exercise representations across heterogeneous knowledge concepts and exercises. For instance, Y ang et al. proposed GIKT [ 27], which utilizes high-order bipartite graphs like “student-exercise”and “student-knowledge concept”to model interactions consistently. Song et al. introduced the “reasoning-generation”model JKT [ 28], which constructs exercise and knowledge concept graphs and integrates their intrinsic relationships through GCN infor- mation propagation. Mao et al. proposed KSGAN [ 29], which predicts student performance at the exercise level by introducing graph attention layers and optimizing representations. KT-Deeper [30] utilizes dynamic reinforced heterogeneous graph contrastive networks to pre- dict students’ cognitive states. It models relationships using temporal heterogeneous graphs and employs reinforcement learning to enhance incomplete graph sequences. Additionally, Song et al. [ 31] proposed a self-supervised knowledge tracing framework, Bi-CLKT, which leverages bi-graph contrastive learning to capture global and local "Exercise-to-Exercise" (E2E) and "Concept-to-Concept" (C2C) relationships, enhancing the model’s performance and interpretability. 2.3 Transfer learning-based knowledge tracing method In real-world scenarios, KT often encounters challenges such as missing data, cold start, and domain discrepancies, which constrain the effectiveness of traditional KT models. In response to these domain-speciﬁc limitations and data sparsity issues, Cheng et al. [ 8] introduced the three-step AdaptKT strategy. This approach facilitates effective knowledge transfer across domains by leveraging Maximum Mean Discrepancy (MMD) [ 32] to minimize distribution discrepancies between different domains and ﬁne-tune the output layer. Huang et al. [9], on the other hand, proposed the STAN framework to address distribution shifts between domains, such as those between different courses, which often degrade model performance when directly applied across domains. They achieve alignment of difﬁculty 123

--- p.5 chars=2544 ---
World Wide Web (2025) 28 :10 Page 5 of 25 10 distributions across domains through conditional adversarial learning and difﬁculty-based sampling methods. However, these methods often overlook the implicit graph topology information present in intermediate layer embeddings and lack detailed pre-training tasks to explicitly model graph topology alignment, relying instead on sequential encoding mech- anisms for embeddings. Recent studies [ 33, 34] have emphasized the critical role of GNNs in explicitly capturing the topological structure of data, exploring both local and global semantics within graphs. This highlights the importance of considering the implicit structure between node embeddings for enhancing KT models. 3 Preliminaries In this section, we ﬁrst review the deﬁnition of knowledge tracing (KT). Then, we explain what cross-domain knowledge tracing (CDKT) is. We have listed some frequently used symbols in Table 1, and provide a detailed explanation of their roles in this context. 3.1 Knowledge tracing KT is a data mining task applied in the ﬁeld of education, which automatically traces the changes in knowledge state based on students’ historical learning records. Speciﬁcally, the KT task can be deﬁned as follows: Given a sequence of historical exercise interactions involving speciﬁc knowledge concepts by students in an online learning system:X = (x 1, x2,..., xt) ={ (e1, y1), (e2, y2) ,...,( et, yt)},w h e r ext = (et, yt) typically represents a tuple, et is the exercise answered at timet,a n d yt ∈{ 0, 1} is the answer label indicating whether the exercise was answered correctly or not (1 for correct, 0 for incorrect). KT aims at predict the probabilitypt+1 = (yt+1 = 1 | et+1, X) of a student correctly answering the next interaction et+1. 3.2 Attribute graph of knowledge tracing To enhance the efﬁcacy of KT predictions, we optimized the reconstruction of model inputs. This involved modeling the multi-dimensional relationships between ‘Exercise to Exercise’ Table 1 A summary of notations Notation Description S; T Source domain; target domain GS; GT Source domain graph; target domain graph V;| V| A set of exercises; the number of exercises n The number of nodes in the graph N(i) The set of neighbors of node i A The structure matrix of graph G ˆA The reconstruction structure matrix of graph G xi The raw feature vector of node i gi The embedding vector of node i during aggregation zi The ﬁnal embedding vector of node i after aggregation ht The hidden knowledge state of a student at time step t 123

--- p.6 chars=3503 ---
10 Page 6 of 25 World Wide Web (2025) 28 :10 (E2E) and ‘Concept to Concept’ (C2C) as separate subgraphs, which were then integrated into a comprehensive knowledge tracing node-attribute graph. In the Exercise to Exercise (E2E) subgraph Ge = (Ve, Ee,w i, j e ),e x e r c i s e sVe are repre- sented as nodes, and edges Ee reﬂect the relationships between these exercises. By analyzing the correctness of students’ responses to different exercises under the same knowledge con- cept, we can calculate the inﬂuencewi, j e (i, j ∈ N, i, j ≤ m) between exercises and construct weighted edges accordingly. The weighted inﬂuence wi, j e between two co-occurring exer- cises is calculated as follows: wi, j e = co_correct (Vi e, V j e )∑ m co_occur (Vie, Vme ), (1) where wi, j e represents the co-correctness rate between exercises Vi e and V j e . This rate denotes the proportion of students who correctly answered both exercises Vi e and V j e among all students who attempted both exercises. It reﬂects the degree of correlation between the two exercises based on student performance. A higher co-correctness rate suggests that if a student answers one exercise correctly, they are also more likely to answer the other correctly. This metric is useful for identifying exercises that are closely related to each other. Before constructing the Concept to Concept (C2C) subgraph, we ﬁrst calculate the average correctness rate of all knowledge concepts in the dataset and compare it with each student’s performance. Students who achieve above-average correctness rates on exercises related to a particular concept are considered to have mastered that concept. Based on this, we then construct the C2C subgraph. Speciﬁcally, in the C2C subgraph Gc = (Vc, Ec,w i, j c ), knowledge concepts Vc are represented as nodes, and edges Ec are determined based on students’ performance on different knowledge concepts. The inﬂuence between knowledge concepts wi, j c is calculated as follows: wi, j c = co_mastery (Vi c, V j c )∑ m co_occur (Vic, Vmc ), (2) where co_mastery denotes the number of students who demonstrate above-average mastery in exercises related to both knowledge concepts.wi, j c represents the co-mastery rate between these two knowledge concepts, which is the proportion of students who exhibit above-average mastery in exercises for both concepts. This co-mastery rate reﬂects the degree of association between the two knowledge concepts, with a higher rate indicating a stronger relationship between the concepts in student learning. The knowledge tracing node-attribute graph consists of a set of student collections U, exercise setVe, student and exercise interaction setEu , and attributes associated with exercises Ae, denoted as G = (U, Ve, Eu, Ae),w h e r eU and Ve serve as vertices, and Eu serve as edges. 3.3 Cross-domain knowledge tracing The objective of CDKT is to align the knowledge state distributions of two domains, effec- tively transferring knowledge from the source domain to the target domain for KT. In the context of transfer learning, we have a more informative source domain S and a target domain T . Given the historical exercise interaction sequence X of a speciﬁc concept for a student, we represent the student’s historical answer records in the source domain S and in the target domain T as X S and X T , respectively. Assuming that we have modeled the multidimensional interaction records between ‘exercise - knowledge concept ’as a graphG, we can describe the 123

--- p.7 chars=2376 ---
World Wide Web (2025) 28 :10 Page 7 of 25 10 graph generated by the ﬁne-tuned model (target domain model) as the target domain graph GT , with node embeddings represented as {xT 1 , xT 2 ,..., xT |V|}; correspondingly, the output generated by the pre-trained model (source domain model) is deﬁned as the source domain graph GS , with node embeddings shown as {xS 1 , xS 2 ,..., xS |V|}. To facilitate effective knowl- edge transfer across domains, a cross-domain alignment module has been designed, treating the cross-domain alignment problem as a graph matching task by matching and adjusting the graph structures of different domains to minimize the differences between the source and target domains in the feature space, making the transfer from GS to GT smoother and more effective. 4 Methodology In this section, we will introduce the overall architecture of AEGOT-CDKT and then elaborate on the details of each module. 4.1 Overview of AEGOT-CDKT framework The overall framework of AEGOT-CDKT is shown in Figure2. This method mainly includes two stages: pre-training and cross-domain prediction, in which the ﬁrst stage is the genera- tion of intra-domain exercises and knowledge concept representations, and the second stage is cross-domain prediction. First, in the initial pre-training phase, we use C2C and E2E subgraphs from the source and target domains to learn and optimize interactions between students and knowledge concepts. and is used to train and boost graph autoencoders, result- ing in more accurate representations of exercises and knowledge concept embeddings. In the cross-domain prediction stage, we use the LSTM model as the basis to integrate the embeddings obtained in the ﬁrst stage with the students’ learning behavior characteristics as the input of the cross-domain prediction model, so as to construct a comprehensive and complementary representation of the students’ knowledge state to predict their performance on the exercises. We then introduce an innovative alignment module within the LSTM to reduce distribution discrepancies between the source and target domains. This module leverages Graph Optimal Transport to minimize the gap between knowledge state distributions across the two domains, facilitating effective knowledge transfer. This method allows similar learning features from Figure 2 The framework structure of AEGOT-CDKT 123

--- p.8 chars=2652 ---
10 Page 8 of 25 World Wide Web (2025) 28 :10 the source domain to successfully transfer to the target domain, aiding students in learning the target domain’s knowledge. Subsequently, we integrate learning features from both the source and target domains to construct a comprehensive and complementary representation of students’ knowledge states. Finally, we enhance the performance of the cross-domain knowledge tracing model by minimizing cross-entropy loss and the optimal transport distance. 4.2 GAT embedding module We referred to the pre-training module of AttGAE-KT [ 35] to obtain initial embeddings of students’ knowledge concepts and exercises. Since the GA T embedding process treats both E2E and C2C subgraphs identically, we will simplify the subsequent discussion by not differentiating between them. Speciﬁcally, the module ﬁrst uses a graph attention network (GA T) as an encoder to aggregate the representations of neighbors with different importance in the subgraphs, calculating the attention scores α i, j between center node i and its neighbor j: αi, j = exp(LeakyReLU(aT [ Wgi ⊕ Wg j ] )) ∑ k∈N(i) exp(LeakyReLU(aT [ Wgi ⊕ Wgk ] )), (3) where W ∈ RF′×F and a ∈ R2F′×1 represents a linear transformation, ⊕ denotes concate- nation operation. g represents the embedding of a node, N(i) represents the set of neighbors of node i, LeakyReLU is an activation function. Then, the features of neighboring nodes j are weighted and summed using two graph attention convolutional layers and attention scoresαi, j , resulting in the aggregated embedding gi of the neighboring nodes of central node i. For the convolutional layers of GA T at layer l = 1 , its calculation formula is: g(1) i = σ ⎛ ⎝ ∑ j∈N(i) α(1) i, j W(1) x(1) j ⎞ ⎠, i = 1, 2,..., n, (4) where σ represents the Sigmoid activation function, x j is the raw feature vector of node j. For the GA TConv layer at layer l = 2, its computation process is as follows: g(2) i = σ ⎛ ⎝ ∑ j∈N(i) α(2) i, j W(2)g(2) j ⎞ ⎠ , i = 1, 2,..., n, (5) Additionally, to further focus on valuable information from multiple vector subspaces, we introduced the multi-head attention mechanism of GA T to independently perform trans- formations and average their layer-wise latent representations. Finally, the results after the second layer output are processed by ReLU and used as the ﬁnal node embeddings. z i = ReLU ⎛ ⎝ 1 H H∑ h=1 ∑ j∈N(i) (α(h) i, j W(2)g(2) j ) ⎞ ⎠ , (6) where H represents the number of parallel attention heads. We used H = 3 attention heads to enhance the model’s learning capability. α(h) i, j denotes the attention coefﬁcient calculated by the h-th attention mechanism. 123

--- p.9 chars=3209 ---
World Wide Web (2025) 28 :10 Page 9 of 25 10 In addition to cross-domain alignment, we use a graph decoding strategy to achieve intra- domain alignment of node embeddings. The motivation for intra-domain alignment is to ensure that students within the same domain who exhibit similar levels of knowledge mas- tery are closer to each other in the embedding space, thus forming more comprehensive and complementary representations. This is crucial for the knowledge tracing task, as students’ learning trajectories span multiple domains and require ﬁne-grained modeling of knowledge mastery within each domain. Through this alignment, the consistency of node embeddings within the same domain is enhanced, allowing for more accurate capture of students’ perfor- mance across different knowledge concepts. We quantify the similarity in knowledge mastery between students by simplifying second- order relationships to ﬁrst-order relationships in the source and target domain graphs. This simpliﬁcation is key in the knowledge tracing task, as it allows us to directly measure the knowledge mastery similarity between two students by embedding their representations in the same space, without considering the complexity of intermediary nodes. Speciﬁcally, if two students exhibit similar levels of mastery for many of the same knowledge concepts, their embeddings are adjusted to be more consistent with each other. To achieve this, we design the following inner product decoder to reconstruct the structure of graph G, without distinguishing between exercises and knowledge concepts for simplicity: ˆAij = σ( zT i z j ), (7) where σ represents the Sigmoid activation function, ˆA represents the reconstructed structure matrix of graph G,a n d z denotes the ﬁnal node embedding obtained after the second graph attention convolutional layer. Finally, we consider graph reconstruction as a binary classiﬁcation task, predicting whether there is a link between two nodes. Formally, we use binary cross-entropy loss as the graph reconstruction loss. Considering that we have both the source domain graph and the target domain graph, the graph decoding loss function for both domains is as follows: Ldec = Lsrc + Ltgt =− 1 N 2 N∑ i=1 N∑ j=1 (AS ij log( ˆA S ij )+(1−As ij ) log(1− ˆA s ij )) − 1 N 2 N∑ i=1 N∑ j=1 (At ij log( ˆA t ij )+(1−At ij ) log(1− ˆA t ij )). (8) 4.3 Prediction module After obtaining the node embeddings of knowledge concepts and exercises from both the source and target domains, we use them as inputs for the prediction module to determine students’ knowledge states in each domain. This prepares the model for cross-domain pre- diction. Our prediction module consists of an LSTM layer and two fully connected layers. We chose the LSTM because it effectively captures the temporal features of students’ learning processes and can dynamically update their knowledge state based on the sequence in which they answer questions. Speciﬁcally, the hidden state of the LSTM is used as the student’s knowledge state because it not only incorporates the historical interactions during the learning process but also integrates the most recent learning behaviors, offering a comprehensive and dynamic 123

--- p.10 chars=3241 ---
10 Page 10 of 25 World Wide Web (2025) 28 :10 representation of the student’s knowledge mastery. To achieve this, we ﬁrst concatenate the knowledge concept embeddings and exercise embeddings obtained through the GA T with the students’ external behavior feature vectors. Then, we use the fully connected layer to merge the features and obtain the input x for the LSTM layer: x i = σ( W1 · (zci ⊕ zei ⊕ fi )) (hlstm t , clstm t ) = LSTM(xt,( hlstm t−1, clstm t−1)), (9) where σ represents the Sigmoid activation function, zei and zci represent the embedding vectors of exercise ei and its corresponding knowledge concept, respectively. xi represents the complete embedding of exercise ei , with each xi corresponding to the input at a speciﬁc time step t. xt represents the input at time step t, hlstm t−1 and clstm t−1 represent the hidden state and cell state of the LSTM unit at time t − 1. fi =[ fi1, fi2,..., fim ] represents the behavioral attributes of a student when completing exercise ei ,w h e r em is the number of features. For the k-th feature in the vector, if it is a categorical feature (exercise type), it is represented in the vector using one-hot encoding as the value f ik ; whereas for numerical features (response time), fik appears as a scalar value. Next, the hidden state hlstm t−1 obtained at time t − 1 is concatenated with the embedding of the exercise qnext completed at at the next time step t to obtain a new feature vector ht : ˜ht =[ hlstm t−1 ⊕ qnext], ht = ReLU(BN(W2 · ˜ht) + rt), (10) where W2 is the weight matrix of the ﬁrst fully connected layer, rt is a portion extracted from the original input xt as the residual input, and BN represents batch normalization. Then, the ﬁnal prediction result is generated through the second fully connected layer: ˆy = W3ht, (11) where W3 is the weight matrix of the second fully connected layer. Finally, we use the cross-entropy loss function to calculate the loss of prediction performance. Lpred =− T∑ t=1 (yt log(ˆyt) + (1 − yt) log(1 − ˆyt)), (12) 4.4 Cross-domain alignment To train an LSTM capable of cross-domain prediction, we begin by acknowledging the need to transfer information from the source domain to the target domain. The model is applied in two scenarios: cross-domain alignment between different stages of the same curriculum and cross-domain alignment of the same curriculum across different schools. In these cases, some knowledge concepts may be shared between the two domains, but their positions within the domain-speciﬁc graphs may vary. While the knowledge concepts and exercises differ across domains, resulting in notable variations in the distribution of knowledge states, there are still underlying connections. These include similarities in the hierarchical structure and logical relationships between knowledge concepts, as well as the difﬁculty and format of exercises. By leveraging these semantic and structural similarities between knowledge concepts and between exercises across domains, we can effectively align knowledge states. Therefore, we use the hidden states of the LSTM as representations of student knowledge states, and cal- culate the Wasserstein Distance (WD) and Gromov-Wasserstein Distance (GWD) separately 123

Data preparation In current research on applying transfer learning to KT, there is a lack of uniﬁed standards for dividing data sets into source and target domains. In order to effectively perform CDKT task, following the settings of transfer learning, we adopt a similar approach to the methods in literature [ 8, 9]. The speciﬁc process is as follows: • First, according to the characteristics of the KT dataset, the data of students, exercises, and knowledge concepts are divided into two independent subsets, representing different source and target domains. Although these two subsets originate from the same original dataset, they exhibit their own characteristic differences in different application scenarios. • Additionally, to speciﬁcally evaluate and validate the effectiveness of knowledge transfer across domains, we considered the inﬂuence of contextual factors in student education 2 https://www.ﬁ.muni.cz/ 3 https://github.com/riiid/ednet 4 https://www.educoder.net/ 123

It utilizes the strong sequential modeling capabilities of RNN, especially LSTM, to dynamically trace and predict the mastery of various knowledge concepts by learners at different time steps. Experimental details Our model is implemented using the PyG 5 framework, and experi- ments were conducted on a Linux server equipped with two NVIDIA A10 GPUs. During the training process, we utilized Adam as the optimizer with a learning rate set to 0.0001, a batch size of 256, and 200 epochs. The model comprises 2 graph attention convolutional layers with 3 attention heads. The embedding dimensions for exercises and knowledge concepts are both 64, while the hidden layer dimension is 128. We adopted the performance evaluation criteria commonly used by most KT institutes, selecting Area Under the Curve (AUC) as the metrics for assessing model performance. AUC measures the area under the ROC curve, plotting true positive rate against false positive rate. It provides an aggregate performance measure across classiﬁcation thresholds. The improvement in AUC values reﬂects the enhancement of the model’s predictive capability. 5.2 Domain transfer experiments (RQ1) Transfer with random partitioning Due to the general lack of speciﬁc background infor- mation for students in the dataset, we adopted the method of randomly partitioning data to construct the transfer learning scenario. Speciﬁcally, we labeled all student data as set U , and selected 20% of the student data as the shared student set, denoted as U shared.T h e remaining student data was divided into two disjoint subsets, denoted as Ud1, Ud2, with Ud1 ∩ Ud2 =∅ . Furthermore, by combining the shared student set with these two subsets, we obtained Ushared ∪Ud1 and Ushared ∪Ud2, which respectively formed the student groups in the source domain and the target domain. This partition strategy aims to simulate cross-domain transfer learning scenarios in the real world, where student groups have both similarities and differences in the source and target domains. In particular, for different transfer task settings: • From Table 6, it can be observed that the AEGOT-CDKT model performs best in CDKT task among randomly grouped students, especially showing signiﬁcant leadership on the Matmat and EdNet-KT1 datasets. • Table7 highlights the strong performance of the AEGOT-CDKT model in the transfer task of “random partition of exercises”, especially in predicting target tasks on the ASSIST09 and Matmat datasets. Its AUC values are notably higher compared to other single-domain models. • Table 8 reveals that single-domain models suffer a substantial decline in cross-domain performance when tackling more intricate knowledge concept transfer issues, owing to their limited transferability. The results indicate that whether it’s student groups, exercises, or knowledge concepts being transferred, the AEGOT-CDKT model is able to more effectively capture and uti- lize source domain information, enhancing learning outcomes in the target domain. This is attributed to the model’s optimization in feature representation, attention mechanisms, and cross-domain alignment, enabling it to have better adaptability and generalization in the ﬁeld of knowledge tracing. In other words, the model can better adapt to new environments and scenarios during the transfer learning process. Transfer based on education context The experiment investigated the effect of model transfer in educational environments, particularly in terms of the ability to predict knowledge states across different schools and course topics. Tables 9 and 10 show the detailed results of the transfer experiments conducted in different school domains and course topic domains. The results of the transfer experiments in the school domain indicate that when the model is applied to predict the knowledge mastery of students across different schools, the AEGOT- CDKT model performs excellently in all transfer scenarios. In particular, in the scenarios of transferring from S1 to S3 and from S3 to S1, the transfer accuracy of the AEGOT-CDKT model is signiﬁcantly higher than that of other reference models. In Table10, we analyzed the transfer effects between different course topics. These course topics, such as C3 (computing quantities), C5 (computing factorials), C2 (modifying lists), and C6 (modifying dictionaries), exhibit logical progression in content. The AEGOT-CDKT model demonstrates commendable performance in transfer learning between these topics. For instance, in the transfer from C2 to C6, i.e., from list modiﬁcation knowledge concepts to dictionary modiﬁcation knowledge concepts, AEGOT-CDKT effectively utilizes the mas- tered knowledge of list operations to facilitate learning of dictionary operations. Thus, this further reﬂects that our proposed cross-domain alignment module can effectively enhance the model’s generalization ability and adaptability across domains. 5.3 Sensitivity analysis (RQ2) In CDKT task, the selection of hyperparameters is paramount as they directly inﬂuence the model’s performance and its ability to generalize on the target domain. Therefore, we assess their impact on the model’s predictive performance by adjusting the values of γ and ξ . γ is crucial for balancing the model’s predictive accuracy on the source domain with the effectiveness of the GOT in transferring knowledge to the target domain. Meanwhile,ξ plays a critical role in adjusting the relative importance of two distinct distance metrics within the GOT framework. To systematically evaluate their effects, we ﬁx one hyperparameter at a time and systematically vary the other across values of 0, 0.25, 0.5, 0.75, and 1. This rigorous evaluation is conducted across three diverse datasets using CDKT task, which partitions knowledge based on concepts. From Figure 3, it can be observed that when γ = 0.25, the AUC score is maximized. This signiﬁes that γ = 0.25 achieves the best cross-domain performance by effectively aligning the knowledge state distributions between the source and target domains, while also ensuring reasonable convergence of training loss in the source domain. An increase in γ diminishes the weight assigned to the GOT, thereby compromising the model’s transferability. Similarly, when ξ = 0.25, the model exhibits optimal performance, indicating that in the ﬁeld of knowledge tracing, the role of Wasserstein Distance (WD) is more important than that of Gromov-Wasserstein Distance (GWD). Speciﬁcally, WD focuses on the absolute distances between knowledge states, which helps ensure semantic matching and alignment of knowledge states. This is crucial for enhancing cross-domain adaptability as it directly aligns the actual content of knowledge. In contrast, GWD focuses on the relative structure between knowledge states, primarily comparing the topological structures of the spaces. Although GWD is useful for capturing the structural information of knowledge states, its impact on overall transfer performance in knowledge tracing tasks is relatively smaller. This suggests that during domain transfer, accurate alignment of semantic information (via WD) is more critical than the alignment of structural information (via GWD). Therefore, WD more effectively captures semantic matching between knowledge states, signiﬁcantly improving the model’s cross-domain adaptability. 5.4 Distribution Alignment Visualization (RQ3) We utilized the T-SNE algorithm [ 38] to conduct dimensionality reduction on the features obtained from different disciplinary domains and course topics for visualization in a two- 123

--- p.20 chars=3943 ---
10 Page 20 of 25 World Wide Web (2025) 28 :10 dimensional space. Combining with the visualization results in Figure 4, it can be observed that the distribution of knowledge states between different domains exhibits a certain degree of dispersion in the two-dimensional space, indicating signiﬁcant differences. Further anal- ysis reveals that, at the course topic level, the distribution distance of students’ knowledge states between C3 (calculating quantities) and C5 (computing factorials) is smaller than that between C1 (string concatenation) and C5. This is because C3 and C5 have logical coherence in their content, whereas C1 and C5 differ signiﬁcantly in both content and skill requirements, resulting in weaker correlation. At the school level, the signiﬁcant differences in students’ learning abilities and habits across different schools lead to larger distribution distances in knowledge states when comparing across schools. After training with the cross-domain alignment module and intra-domain alignment mechanism, the knowledge states of different schools or course topics become more concentrated and similar, with some overlap. This result demonstrates the effectiveness of our proposed method in reducing the discrepancies in knowledge state distribution between different disciplinary domains and course topics, thereby facilitating cross-domain knowledge transfer. 5.5 Stability Analysis (RQ4) In this section, we analyzed the knowledge state changes of students under the transfer and non-transfer scenarios using the AEGOT-CDKT model. Figure 5 illustrates the knowledge state changes of knowledge concepts c32 (positive decimal ordering) and c33 (fraction order- ing) when transferring between schools S1 and S2. The horizontal axis of the knowledge state change graph represents a student’s sequence of interactions, where each time step consists of (concept, answer), indicating the student’s answering situation at a certain time step. The vertical axis lists 2 different knowledge concepts. The depth of color in the graph corresponds to the model’s forecasted probability of a student providing a correct answer. Darker shades signify a greater probability according to the model that the knowledge concept has been correctly understood. We observed that in the non-transfer scenario in Figure 5, the model’s answer predictions exhibited some ﬂuctuations, indicating that the model only utilized fewer data in the target domain S2, neglecting the nonlinear relationships between students, knowledge concepts, and exercises, resulting in a lack of deeper semantic understanding of the students’ knowledge state. After domain transfer, the student’s knowledge state showed a stable trend without any abnormal ﬂuctuations. After answering correctly (or incorrectly) in a concept’s exercise, the corresponding concept mastery level gradually increased (or decreased). It can be observed that in the ﬁrst three exercises of c32 (positive decimal ordering), the student only answered 1 correctly, indicating a relatively low mastery level of “positive decimal ordering” initially; however, in the later 5 exercises of “positive decimal ordering”, the student answered 4 correctly, leading the model to believe that he gradually mastered “positive decimal ordering”. Similarly, although the student’s overall accuracy in c33 (fraction ordering) is relatively high, he answered incorrectly in 2 out of the last 4 exercises of “fraction ordering”, causing the model to determine that the student only partially mastered “fraction ordering” in the end. Overall, the model’s judgments align with our understanding. This phenomenon suggests that the model can improve its knowledge state learning ability in the target domain through the representation of knowledge states in the source domain after transfer. Thus, our approach indeed facilitates knowledge state sharing and helps the model better predict in new domains. 123

--- p.23 chars=3460 ---
World Wide Web (2025) 28 :10 Page 23 of 25 10 6 Conclusions This paper presents a cross-domain knowledge tracing model based on transfer learning called AEGOT-CDKT, aiming to bridge the knowledge gap between different domains while optimizing and enhancing the performance of knowledge tracing in new domains. The core innovation of AEGOT-CDKT lies in introducing an alignment module capable of handling and reducing the distributional differences of learners’ knowledge states across different domains. Moreover, it applies Graph Optimal Transport algorithm to effectively align the graph structures of different domains, thus enhancing the complementarity of student knowl- edge state representations and providing learners with more precise learning guidance. Finally, experimental studies and visual analyses are conducted to validate the models excellent performance in various target domains, further demonstrating the crucial role of transfer learning in improving the generalization and adaptability of knowledge tracing mod- els.
